Greek (Kühn)

νυνὶ δ’ ἐφ’ ἕτερον γένος τῶν πολυχρήστων ἐμπλάστρων μεταβήσομαι πρὸς ἄλλα τε πολλὰ ποιούσης ἑκάστης καὶ μέντοι καὶ πρὸς τὰ σηπόμενα τῶν μορίων,  ἑτέρας ὄντα διαθέσεως, ταῦτα τῶν ἰδίως δυσθεραπεύτων ὀνομαζομένων ἑλκῶν. ἐπὶ μὲν γὰρ τούτων ἡ σηπεδὼν ἐπινέμεται καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καλοῦσι τὰς διαθέσεις ταύτας οἱ πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν νομὰς, ἐπ’ ἐκείνων δὲ περιγέγραπται καθ’ ἓν χωρίον ἄνευ σηπεδόνος, ὥστε καὶ τὰς φαγεδαίνας ὀνομαζομένας ἀνεσθίειν μὲν τὰ συνεχῆ, χωρὶς μέντοι τοῦ σήπειν αὐτὰ καὶ πυρετοὺς ἐπιφέρειν, ὅπερ εἴωθεν ἀκολουθεῖν τοῖς μετὰ σηπεδόνος νομώδεσι. κεφάλαιον δὲ τῆς ἰάσεως ἦν, ἐπὶ μὲν τοῦ προειρημένου γένους τῶν δυσθεραπεύτων ἑλκῶν, ἃ καὶ χειρώνεια καλοῦσιν, εὑρεῖν φάρμακα, καθόσον οἷόν τε μὴ δάκνοντα μὲν, ἰσχυρῶς δὲ ξηραίνοντα. ταυτὶ δὲ τὰ νῦν γραφησόμενα δακνώδη πάντ’ ἐστὶν, εἴ τις αὐτοῖς ἀκράτοις ἄνευ τοῦ μιχθῆναι κηρωτῇ χρῷτο. μιγνύμενα δὲ κηρωτῇ καθῇραι μὲν ἕλκος ῥυπαρὸν καὶ σαρκῶσαι δύναται καί τινα καὶ διαφορῆσαι σκληρότητα καὶ τάσιν, οὐ μὴν τὰ δυσθεράπευτά γε ἰᾶσθαι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But now I shall pass to another kind of the plasters of many uses (polychrēsta), each of which acts upon many other things and indeed also upon the putrefying parts,  these being of a different condition from the ulcers that are properly called hard-to-treat. For in the former the putrefaction spreads, and for this reason most physicians call these conditions nomai (spreading sores); whereas in the latter it is circumscribed in a single place without putrefaction, so that even the so-called phagedainai eat away the adjoining parts, yet without putrefying them and bringing on fevers, which is what usually follows upon the spreading sores accompanied by putrefaction. The chief point of the cure was, in the case of the previously mentioned kind of hard-to-treat ulcers, which they also call Chironian, to find drugs that, as far as possible, do not bite, but dry strongly. But these which are now to be written down are all biting, if one were to use them unmixed, without their being mixed with cerate. When mixed with cerate they are able to cleanse a foul ulcer and to produce flesh, and also to disperse a certain hardness and tension, yet not to cure the hard-to-treat ones.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: νομάς, νομώδεσι, χειρώνεια

Now I shall pass on to another kind of polychrest plasters, each of which acts against many other things and, indeed, also against the putrefying parts,  these being of a different condition from the ulcers specifically called hard-to-treat. For in the former the putrefaction spreads, and for this reason most physicians call these conditions nomai (spreading sores); in the latter, however, the condition is circumscribed in a single place without putrefaction, so that even the so-called phagedaenas eat away the contiguous parts, yet without making them putrefy and bringing on fevers, which usually follows the spreading conditions accompanied by putrefaction. The chief point of the cure, in the case of the aforementioned kind of hard-to-treat ulcers, which they also call Chironian, was to find drugs that, as far as possible, do not bite but dry strongly. But the drugs now to be written down are all biting if one uses them unmixed, without their being mixed with cerate. Mixed with cerate, however, they are able to cleanse a foul ulcer and fill it with flesh, and also to disperse some hardness and tension, but not to cure the hard-to-treat ones.

Greek (Kühn)

δηλοῖ δ’ αὐτῶν τὴν δύναμιν τὰ πάθη πρὸς ἃ πεπίστευται παραλαμβάνεσθαι καλῶς· ὃ καὶ κατὰ τὸν ἔμπροσθεν εἴρηται λόγον καὶ νῦν ἐρῶ, μεμνῆσθαι  χρὴ τῶν γεγραμμένων ἐν τῇ περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων πραγματείᾳ, κατὰ μὲν τὰ πρῶτα βιβλία τὴν μέθοδον εἰπόντος μου, καθ’ ἣν εὑρίσκονται τῶν φαρμάκων αἱ δυνάμεις, μετὰ τοῦ καὶ γυμνάσασθαι κατ’ αὐτὴν ἐπὶ παραδειγμάτων πλειόνων, ἐν δὲ τοῖς ἑξῆς ἑκάστου κατ’ ὄνομα μνημονεύσαντος. ὁ γὰρ ἐκείνων μεμνημένος εὐκόλως παρακολουθήσει τῷ λογισμῷ τῶν συνθέντων ταυτὶ τὰ φάρμακα τὰ νῦν ἡμῖν προκείμενα καὶ δηλονότι καὶ αὐτὸς ἕτερα συνθήσει, καθ’ ὃν ἂν χρόνον ἀπορῇ τῶν ἤδη προπεπειραμένων. πολλὰ γὰρ τοιαῦτα συμβαίνει πολλοῖς ἐν ἀποδημίαις, ταῖς τε ἄλλαις καὶ μάλιστα ταῖς κατὰ πλοῦν, ὅπου γε καὶ κατὰ χώραν μενόντων ἀπώλετό τινων ἡ παρασκευὴ τῶν φαρμάκων, ἐξ ὧν ἐθεράπευον τοὺς δεομένους, ὥσθ’ ὑπὸ λύπης αὐτοὺς συντακέντας ἀποθανεῖν. ἄριστον μὲν οὖν ἤδη τοῖς πεῖραν δεδωκόσι φαρμάκοις χρῆσθαι κατὰ τὴν εἰρημένην μέθοδον.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

Their power is shown by the affections for which they are trusted to be employed with good effect; and what was said in the preceding discussion I shall say now as well: one must remember  what has been written in the treatise on simple drugs, where in the first books I set out the method by which the powers of drugs are discovered, together with exercising oneself in it on a good many examples, and in the following books I made mention of each one by name. For one who remembers those things will easily follow the reasoning of those who composed these drugs now set before us, and will obviously himself also compose others at whatever time he is at a loss for those already tried beforehand. For many such things happen to many people on journeys abroad, both other journeys and especially those by sea, seeing that even while remaining in their own place some have lost the stock of drugs by which they used to treat those in need, so that they wasted away from grief and died. It is best, then, to use drugs that have already given proof of themselves, in accordance with the method described.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὅπου γε, μετὰ τοῦ καὶ γυμνάσασθαι

Their power is made plain by the affections for which they are trusted to be properly employed. And, as was said in the earlier discussion and as I shall say now as well, one must remember  what was written in the treatise on simple drugs: in its first books I stated the method by which the powers of drugs are discovered, and also exercised myself in it on a number of examples, while in the books that follow I recorded each drug by name. For whoever remembers those things will easily follow the reasoning of those who composed these drugs that are now before us, and he will clearly also compose others himself, at whatever time he lacks those already tested. For many such things happen to many people on journeys abroad, both on other kinds and above all on those by sea; indeed, even with some who stayed at home, the stock of drugs from which they treated those in need was lost, so that they wasted away from grief and died. Best, then, is to use drugs that have already given proof, in accordance with the method stated.

Greek (Kühn)

εἰ δ’, ὡς ἔφην, ἀπορήσαιμέν ποτε τούτων, ἕξιν ἔχειν αὐτοὺς τοιαύτην, ὡς δύνασθαι συντιθέναι παραπλήσια τοῖς ἤδη δεδωκόσιν ἱκανὴν πεῖραν, ὥσπερ πολλάκις κᾀμὲ ὁρᾶτε πράττοντα  κατά γε τὰς ἀποδημίας καὶ ἄλλως. ἔνια γοῦν τῶν αὐτοσχεδιασθέντων ἐν τοιαύταις περιστάσεσι βασανισθέντα τῇ πείρᾳ τῶν ἐνδοξοτάτων οὐδὲν ἐφάνη χείρω. τὴν μὲν γὰρ πρώτην αὐτῶν σύνθεσιν ἐκ στοχασμοῦ τεχνικοῦ ποιούμεθα, μετὰ ταῦτα δὲ ἀπὸ τῆς πείρας, ἐνίων μὲν φανέντων δοκιμωτάτων, ἐνίων δ’ ἀπολειπομένων τοῦ σκοποῦ, δευτέρα πάλιν ἐπανόρθωσις γίνεται. καὶ τρίτη δ’ ἐνίοτε πρὸς τὸ τέλειον ἄγουσα τῆς ἐπαγγελίας αὐτά. χωρὶς δὲ τῆς εἰρημένης μεθόδου κατά τε τὰ πρόσθεν ὑπομνήματα καὶ κατὰ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς πραγματείας, χρήσασθαί τε τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν εἰρημένοις ὀρθῶς ἀδύνατον, αὐτόν τε συνθεῖναι παραπλησίως ἐκείνοις ἔτι γε ἀδυνατώτερον. ἤδη τοίνυν ἄρξομαι τῶν τοιούτων ἐμπλάστρων. τὴν διὰ βοτανῶν γεγράφασιν ἅπαντες σχεδὸν οἱ τὰς ἐνδόξους ἐμπλάστρους ἀθροίσαντες ἐν γραφαῖς βίβλου μιᾶς ἢ πλειόνων, οὐ πολὺ διαφερόμενοι τῇ συμμετρίᾳ τῶν μιγνυμένων φαρμάκων ἁπλῶν. ἄρξομαι δὴ κᾀνταῦθα τῶν τῷ Κρίτωνι γεγραμμένων, πρῶτον μνημονεύων ἃν Κρίτων ἔγραψεν ἐμπλάστρους ἐν τῷ δ΄. περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων αὐτῇ λέξει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἃν, ἐν τῷ δ΄. περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων, ἕξιν ἔχειν αὐτοὺς

But if, as I said, we should ever be at a loss for these, [it is best] that they themselves possess such a habit as to be able to compose drugs resembling those that have already given sufficient proof, just as you often see me too doing,  both on journeys abroad and otherwise. At any rate, some of the ones improvised in such circumstances, when tested by experience, appeared in no way inferior to the most renowned. For we make their first composition from technical conjecture, and after this, on the basis of experience—some having appeared most approved, others falling short of the aim—a second correction in turn comes about, and sometimes also a third, bringing them to the perfection of what they promise. But without the method described both in the preceding commentaries and in the therapeutic treatise, it is impossible to use correctly the drugs mentioned before, and still more impossible to compose oneself drugs resembling those. Now then I shall begin with plasters of this sort. The plaster made of herbs has been written up by almost all who have collected the renowned plasters in the writings of one book or of several, not differing much in the proportion of the simple drugs that are mixed. Here too, then, I shall begin with those written by Crito, first mentioning the plasters which Crito wrote in the fourth book On Simple Drugs, in his very words.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κατὰ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς πραγματείας (genitive after κατά; probably for κατὰ τὴν), ἃν (probably for ἃς), ἐν τῷ δ΄. περὶ τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων (attribution of this title to Crito uncertain), ἕξιν ἔχειν αὐτοὺς (infinitive construction elliptical; governing 'it is best' supplied)

But if, as I said, we should at some time lack these, [it is best] that we ourselves have such a trained disposition as to be able to compound drugs similar to those that have already given sufficient proof by experience, as you often see me doing too,  at least on journeys abroad, and in other circumstances as well. At any rate, some of the drugs improvised in such circumstances, when tested by experience, proved in no way inferior to the most renowned. For we make their first composition from skilled estimation; after that, on the basis of experience, since some prove most excellent while others fall short of the aim, a second correction is made in turn; and sometimes a third as well, which brings them to the full accomplishment of what they promise. Without the method stated both in the earlier treatises and in the therapeutic treatise, it is impossible to use correctly the drugs described previously, and still more impossible to compound oneself drugs similar to them. Now then I shall begin on plasters of this kind. The plaster made with herbs has been written up by nearly all who have collected the renowned plasters in writings of one book or of several, and they do not differ much in the proportion of the simple drugs mixed in. Here too, then, I shall begin with those written by Crito, first mentioning, in his very words, the plasters that Crito wrote up in the fourth book On Simple Drugs.

Greek (Kühn)

 [Ἡ διὰ βοτανῶν Κρίτωνος.] Ἡ διὰ βοτανῶν, ᾗ χρῶμαι, ποιεῖ πρὸς καρκινώματα, σηπεδόνας· χοιράδας εἰς διαπύησιν ἄγουσα καὶ ἐξιποῦσα λέπρας ἀφίστησι καὶ δοθιῆνας διαλύει, ποδαγρικοῖς ἁρμόζει, πώρους διαλύει, ἡ αὐτὴ κεφαλικὴ ἄνευ ἀνατρήσεως λεπίδας ἀφίστησιν, ἀνάγει ὀστᾶ, ἐπιξύει δὲ τὰ ὀστᾶ ἐπὶ ποσὸν, ἡ αὐτὴ πρὸς τὰ νεότρωτα πρὸς νομὰς ἔμμοτος ἀνακαθαίρει, πληροῖ σὺν ῥοδίνῃ κηρωτῇ ἑνὸς μέρους πρὸς δύο κηρωτῆς μέρη, ἐνίοτε δὲ γ΄ ἢ δ΄. μιγνυμένου. σὺν δὲ μυρσίνῃ κηρωτῇ δυσὶ μέρεσιν ἣ τρισὶν ἐπουλοῖ. ♃ βοτανῶν ἐξ ἀνύδρων τόπων παραθαλασσίων, ἀναγαλλίδος τῆς τὸ κυανοῦν ἄνθος ἐχούσης δραχ. νστ΄. μήκωνος κερατίτιδος δραχ. νστ΄. πρασίου δραχ. νστ΄. ὑοσκυάμου φύλλων ἁπαλῶν δραχ. νστ΄. ταύτας εἰς ὅλμον βαλὼν, κόπτε ἕως θλασθῇ καὶ διαλυθῇ, μόνον μὴ τὸν χυλὸν αὐτῶν δαπανήσῃς, ἔπειτα βαλὼν εἰς θυείαν τρῖβε. ἐν ἄλλῃ δὲ θυείᾳ ἔχε λεπίδος ἐρυθροῦ χαλκοῦ δραχ. κε΄. μάννης δραχ. η΄. ῥητίνης πιτυΐνης ξηρᾶς δραχ. ιστ΄. ἀλόης δραχ. ιστ΄. σχιστῆς δραχ. δ΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐξιποῦσα, σχιστῆς, μάννης

 [The plaster through herbs, of Crito.] The plaster through herbs, which I use, works against carcinomata and putrefactions; it brings scrofulous swellings (choirades) to suppuration and, pressing them out, removes leprai and dissolves boils (dothienes); it is suited to gouty patients and dissolves calluses (poroi); the same is a cephalic plaster, which without trepanation detaches scales of bone, brings up bones, and scrapes the bones to some extent; the same, for freshly inflicted wounds and for spreading sores (nomai), applied on lint, cleanses thoroughly and fills up, together with rose cerate, one part being mixed to two parts of cerate, and sometimes three or four; and with myrtle cerate, in two or three parts, it cicatrizes. Take of herbs from waterless places near the sea: of the pimpernel which has the blue flower 56 drachms, of horned poppy 56 drachms, of horehound 56 drachms, of tender leaves of henbane 56 drachms; put these into a mortar and pound them until they are crushed and broken down, only do not waste their juice; then put them into a mortar-basin and grind. And in another mortar-basin have of scale of red copper 25 drachms, of manna 8 drachms, of dry pine resin 16 drachms, of aloe 16 drachms, of fissile (alum, schiste) 4 drachms,

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐξιποῦσα, ἐπιξύει, ἔμμοτος, σχιστῆς, μάννης, κεφαλικὴ

 [The plaster from herbs of Criton.] The plaster from herbs, which I use, is effective against carcinomas and putrefactions; bringing choirades to suppuration and squeezing them out, it removes lepras and dissolves boils; it is suitable for gouty patients; it dissolves calluses; the same, as a cephalic, removes scales of bone without trepanation, brings up bones, and scrapes the bones to some extent; the same is for freshly inflicted wounds; for spreading ulcers, applied on lint, it cleans them out; it fills in with rose cerate, one part being mixed to two parts of cerate, and sometimes to three or four. With myrtle cerate, in two or three parts, it brings to a scar. ♃ Herbs from waterless places by the sea: pimpernel, the kind that has the dark-blue flower, 56 dr.; horned poppy 56 dr.; horehound 56 dr.; tender leaves of henbane 56 dr. Throw these into a mortar and pound until they are crushed and broken down, only do not waste their juice; then throw them into a grinding-mortar and triturate. In another grinding-mortar have: scale of red copper 25 dr.; manna 8 dr.; dry pine resin 16 dr.; aloe 16 dr.; split [alum] 4 dr.

Ἥρας οὕτως. τινὲς δὲ τὰς δύο ἀναγαλλίδας βάλλουσι, τινὲς δὲ καὶ χρυσοκόλλης καὶ γῆς Ἐρετριάδος καὶ ἁλὸς ἀμμωνιακοῦ καὶ ἀρσενικοῦ, σχιστοῦ ἀνὰ δραχ. δ΄. ἃ δὲ περὶ τῆς ἐμπλάστρου ταύτης Ἀσκληπιάδης γέγραφε καὶ ἐν τῷ πρὸ τούτου βιβλίῳ δεδήλωται, παραπλήσιά πως ὄντα τούτοις, διὸ καὶ περιττὸν ἔδοξέ μοι γράφειν αὖθις αὐτὰ, μέλλοντί γε τὸν κοινὸν λόγον ὑπὲρ αὐτῶν διέρχεσθαι νῦν. ὁ γάρ τοι πρῶτος συνθεὶς τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο, βοτάναις ἐχρήσατο δριμείαις εἰς τὸ διαφορῆσαι σφοδρῶς τοὺς ἐν τοῖς πεπονθόσι μέρεσι μοχθηροὺς χυμοὺς, διὰ τοῦτο γοῦν καὶ ἐξ ἀνύδρων καὶ παραθαλασσίων τόπων εἶναι κελεύει, ἐναντίας δ’ αὐταῖς ἄλλας ψυχρᾶς δυνάμεως, ἕνεκα τοῦ παρηγορῆσαι τὸ σφοδρὸν τῶν δριμέων, οἷαί πέρ εἰσιν ὅ τε ὑοσκύαμος καὶ ἡ μήκων.

 of round (alum), 4 drachmas. ἔχουσι γάρ τι καὶ πραϋντικὸν ὀδυνῶν, ναρκώδη ψύξιν ἐντιθεῖσαι  τοῖς πλησιάζουσι. νάρκη δὲ μετρίη ὀδύνης λυτικὴ, φησὶν Ἱπποκράτης. προσέθηκε δὲ καὶ τῶν μεταλλικῶν τήν τε λεπίδα τοῦ χαλκοῦ καὶ τὴν σχιστὴν στυπτηρίαν, ὑπὲρ ὧν ἔμπροσθεν εἶπον, ὅπερ ἑκάτερον τῶν φαρμάκων ἁρμόττει κακοήθεσιν ἕλκεσιν. ἀλόην δ’ ἐκ τῶν στυφόντων καὶ ξηραινόντων ἀδήκτως φαρμάκων ἔμιξε καὶ τὴν μάνναν τῶν πεσσόντων τε καὶ παρηγορούντων καὶ διαπυϊσκόντων. τὴν δὲ πιτυΐνην ὡς ὕλην εἰς ἐμπλάστρου γένεσιν, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τὸν κηρὸν καὶ τὸ ἔλαιον, ἅμα δὲ καὶ τούτῳ συμβαίνει πραΰνεσθαι δι’ αὐτῶν τὸ σφοδρὸν τῶν δριμέων φαρμάκων. ἥ γε μὴν πιτυΐνη ῥητίνη καὶ τὸ ξηραίνειν ἔχει καὶ ὅσῳ περ ἂν ξηροτέρα τοσούτῳ μᾶλλον οἵαν περ ἀξιοῖ βάλλειν. ἐνίους δέ φησι μεμιχέναι τῷ φαρμάκῳ καὶ χρυσοκόλλης καὶ γῆς Ἐρετριάδος, ἀμφοτέρων τὰ δυσθεράπευτα τῶν ἑλκῶν ἰωμένων. ἔμπαλιν δὲ τούτοις ἅλες ἀμμωνιακοὶ καὶ τὸ καλούμενον ἀρσενικὸν ἐρεθίζειν ἕλκη πέφυκεν ὑπὸ δριμύτητος, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς σηπεδονώδεσιν ἁρμόττει.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: οἵαν περ ἀξιοῖ βάλλειν

For they also have something that mitigates pains, since they impart a numbing coldness  to the parts they come near. And moderate numbness relieves pain, says Hippocrates. He also added, from the metallic substances, both flake of copper and fissile alum, about which I spoke earlier, each of which drugs is suited to malignant ulcers. Aloe he mixed in from among the drugs that are astringent and dry without biting, and manna from among those that concoct, soothe and promote suppuration. The pine resin he added as material for the formation of a plaster, just as he did the wax and the oil, while at the same time it also comes about through these that the vehemence of the acrid drugs is mitigated. Pine resin, however, also has the property of drying, and the drier it is, the more so—of the kind he thinks fit to put in. He says that some have also mixed into the drug chrysocolla and Eretrian earth, both of which heal the hard-to-treat ulcers. Contrary to these, ammoniac salt and what is called arsenic are of such a nature as to irritate ulcers by their acridity, yet they are also suited to putrefying ones.

For they also have something mitigating of pains, implanting a numbing chill  in whatever they come near. 'Moderate numbness is a resolver of pain,' says Hippocrates. Of the metallic substances he also added the scale of copper and the split alum, about which I spoke earlier, namely that each of these two drugs suits malignant ulcers. Aloe he mixed in from among the drugs that are astringent and dry without biting, and manna from among those that concoct, soothe and bring to suppuration. The pine resin he added as material for the making of a plaster, just as he did the wax and the oil; at the same time it also comes about through these that the vehemence of the acrid drugs is tempered. The pine resin, however, also has a drying property, and the drier it is, the more it is of the kind he requires to be put in. He says that some have also mixed into the drug chrysocolla and Eretrian earth, since both heal the hard-to-treat ulcers. Conversely to these, ammoniac salts and what is called arsenikon are by nature such as to irritate ulcers on account of their acridity, but they are also suited to putrefying ones.

Greek (Kühn)

καὶ διὰ τοῦτο πρὸς μὲν τὰ χειρώνεια τῶν ἑλκῶν οὐ πάνυ τι χρήσιμόν ἐστι τὸ προκείμενον  ἐν τῷ λόγῳ φάρμακον. ἐφ’ ὧν δὲ σηπεδών τις ὑπάρχει, παῦσαι ταύτην ἐπιτήδειόν ἐστι, διαφοροῦν τοὺς ἐργαζομένους αὐτὴν ἰσχῶρας. ἡ δ’ αὐτὴ δύναμις τοῦ φαρμάκου καὶ τὰς χοιράδας ἰᾶται. ἐπεὶ δὲ ῥυπτικά τινα τῶν ἐμβεβλημένων ἐστὶν ἐν αὐτῷ, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ λέπρας καθαίρει. σκοπὸς μὲν γὰρ ἐπὶ τοῦ παθήματος τούτου τὸ οἷον ἀπολεπίσαι τὸ κατεσκληρυσμένον τοῦ δέρματος. ἐργάζεται δὲ τοῦτο τὰ ὑπερξηραίνοντα μετὰ τοῦ ῥύπτειν. εἰ μὲν γὰρ εἰς τὸ κατὰ φύσιν ἐπανελθεῖν ἠδύνατο τοῦ δέρματος ἡ διάθεσις, οὐκ ἄν τις τὸ κατεξηρασμένον ὑπερεξήραινεν. ἐπεὶ δὲ οὐχ οἷόν τέ ἐστι τὸ ἤδη λεπρῶδες ἰαθῆναι, δεύτερος ἂν εἴη σκοπὸς, ὅσπερ ἐπὶ πάντων τῶν ἀνιάτων ἐστὶ κοινὸς, ἐκκόψαι. τοῦ σώματος τὸ ἀνίατον. ἐκκόπτεται δὲ ποτὲ μὲν σιδήρῳ, ποτὲ δὲ πυρὶ, ποτὲ δὲ φαρμάκῳ ἤτοι καυστικοῖς ἢ διαφορητικοῖς τε ἅμα καὶ ῥυπτικοῖς, ὁποῖόν ἐστι τὸ προκείμενον νῦν. ὥσπερ οὖν οὗτος ὁ λόγος κοινός ἐστιν ἐπὶ πάσης λέπρας, οὕτως καὶ ὁ περὶ τῆς σαρκώσεως.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And for this reason, against the Cheironian ulcers the drug set before us  in the discussion is not very useful. But in cases where some putrefaction is present, it is suitable for putting a stop to this, since it disperses the ichors that produce it. The same power of the drug also cures the scrofulous swellings (choirades). And since some of the ingredients thrown into it are detergent, for this reason it also cleanses leprai. For the aim in the case of this affection is, as it were, to scale off the hardened part of the skin; and this is accomplished by things that over-dry together with cleansing. For if the condition of the skin were able to return to the state according to nature, no one would over-dry what has already been dried out. But since it is not possible for what is already leprous to be healed, the second aim would be, the one that is common in the case of all incurable things, to cut out the incurable part of the body. And it is cut out sometimes by iron, sometimes by fire, sometimes by a drug, either caustics or those that are at once discutient and detergent, such as the one now set before us. Just as, then, this account is common to every lepra, so too is the one concerning the production of flesh (sarkosis).

Just as, then, this account is common to every lepra, so too is the account concerning the generation of flesh.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπεὶ γὰρ, ὡς ἐν τῷ τρίτῳ τῆς θεραπευτικῆς μεθόδου δέδεικται, τὰ σαρκωτικὰ καλούμενα φάρμακα δυνάμεώς ἐστιν ἀδήκτου  ῥυπτικῆς. ὅσα δὲ τῆς ὕλης ταύτης ἐστὶν, εἰς τουτὶ τὸ νῦν ἡμῖν προκείμενον ἐν τῷ λόγῳ φάρμακον, ἔχει μὲν τὸ ῥυπτικὸν, οὐκ ἔχει δὲ τὸ ἄδηκτον· διὰ τοῦτο μίγνυμεν αὐτοῖς ὑγρὰν κηρωτὴν ἐκ κηροῦ καὶ ῥητίνης συγκειμένην τετηκότων ἐν ἐλαίῳ. ὁ μὲν γὰρ κηρὸς καὶ τὸ ἔλαιον ὥσπερ ἀμβλυτικὰ δριμυτήτων ἐστὶν, οὕτως ἐμπλαστικὰ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ῥύπου γεννητικά. ἡ ῥητίνη δὲ τὸ ἄδηκτον ἔχουσα τὸ ἐμπλαστικὸν οὐκ ἔχει, ῥυπτικῆς οὖσα δυνάμεως ἕλκεσι μὲν ἤδη δακνώδους, τοῖς δὲ ἀνελκώτοις ἀδήκτου. τὴν δ’ ἐν τῇ μίξει συμμετρίαν στοχαστικῶς μὲν πρὸ τῆς πείρας, ἐπιστημονικῶς δὲ μετὰ τὴν πεῖραν εὑρήσομεν ἅμα τῷ γινώσκειν, ὅτι τὸ μὲν καθαρὸν ἕλκος ἀκριβῶς ἀδήκτου δεῖται δυνάμεως τοῦ ῥυπτικοῦ, τὸ δὲ ῥυπαρὸν ἰσχυροτέρου. τούτου γὰρ ὁ ῥύπος ἐστὶ πρόβλημα, τὸ καθαρὸν δὲ γυμνὸν ὁμιλεῖ τῷ ψαύοντι. καὶ μέντοι καὶ τῶν σωμάτων αὐτῶν τὰ μὲν αἰσθητικωτέραν ἔχει τὴν οὐσίαν τὰ δὲ δυσαισθητικωτέραν, καὶ τὰ μὲν μαλακωτέραν, τὰ δὲ σκληροτέραν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πρόβλημα, ὅσα δὲ τῆς ὕλης ταύτης ἐστὶν, εἰς τουτὶ

For since, as has been shown in the third book of the Therapeutic Method, the drugs called flesh-producing are of a non-biting  cleansing power, while all the materials that enter into this drug now before us in our discussion have the cleansing quality but do not have the non-biting quality, for this reason we mix with them a liquid cerate composed of wax and resin (pine-resin) melted in oil. For wax and oil, just as they are blunters of acridities, so too are they pore-blocking (emplastic) and for this reason generative of filth. Resin, on the other hand, while it has the non-biting quality, does not have the pore-blocking one, being of a cleansing power that is already biting for ulcers but non-biting for unulcerated parts. The due proportion in the mixture we shall find conjecturally before experience, but scientifically after experience, together with the recognition that the clean ulcer requires a cleansing power that is precisely non-biting, whereas the filthy one requires a stronger one. For in the latter the filth is a screen set before it, while the clean one, being bare, comes into contact with whatever touches it. And moreover, of the bodies themselves, some have a more sensitive substance, others a less sensitive one, and some a softer, others a harder.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐπεὶ γὰρ ... ἐστιν ἀδήκτου ῥυπτικῆς. (anacoluthon: the 'since' clause has no main clause before the full stop; taken as continuing into the next sentence), εἰς τουτὶ τὸ νῦν ἡμῖν προκείμενον ἐν τῷ λόγῳ φάρμακον (no verb; 'go into' supplied), πρόβλημα (taken as 'protective covering')

For since, as was shown in the third book of the Therapeutic Method, the drugs called flesh-producing are of a non-biting  detergent faculty; and since those of this class of material that go into the drug now set before us in the discussion have the detergent property but do not have the non-biting one, for this reason we mix with them a liquid cerate composed of wax and resin melted in oil. For wax and oil, just as they blunt acridities, are likewise emplastic, and for this reason generative of filth. Resin, on the other hand, though it has the non-biting property, does not have the emplastic one, being of a detergent faculty that is biting to parts already ulcerated but non-biting to unulcerated ones. The due proportion in the mixture we shall find conjecturally before trial, but scientifically after trial, knowing at the same time that the clean ulcer requires a detergent of an exactly non-biting faculty, the dirty one a stronger detergent. For in the latter the filth serves as a protective covering, whereas the clean ulcer meets bare whatever touches it. And moreover, of the bodies themselves, some have a more sensitive substance, others a less sensitive one, and some a softer, others a harder.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For this reason, then, Criton too, in the case of the drug under discussion, orders that to one part  of it there be mixed sometimes two parts of the cerate, sometimes three or four; he did not, however, define when one ought to mix the two parts and when the more, on the assumption that the person who is going to use the drug already has some practice in the art and is able to reason out what is now being said by me. It is better, to be sure, for the cerate to have been prepared with rose-oil, though the superiority is not great in such ulcers as these, even if it were melted with olive oil. For rose-oil is indeed better than olive oil in cases that have something inflammatory about them, but the ulcers that are going to be filled with flesh are in every case free of inflammation. And Criton rightly added the note that in the case of ulcers requiring cicatrization the cerate ought to have been prepared with myrtle-oil, for those have need of astringency. This much suffices to have been said, for the present at least, about the flesh-producing and the cicatrizing drugs. For the method of their use and composition has been stated in the books of the Therapeutic Method; while the exercise upon the particular materials, which belongs to the present treatise, will be stated in the book after this one.

 [Plaster with squill.] Next after this one Crito wrote another plaster of the same faculty by kind, in these very words, as follows. ♃ of chalcitis, of round aristolochia, 30 drachms each; others, 15 drachms; of iris 30 drachms; others, 15 drachms; of dry pine resin 30 drachms; others, 15 drachms; of ammoniac 15 drachms; of myrrh 15 drachms; of storax 15 drachms; of scale of red copper 15 drachms; of roasted squill 108 drachms; sharpest vinegar: triturate for 40 days in the heat of the dog days. First the scale and the storax, a little at a time from each of them; then, having added the ammoniac and the copper and the squill, so that it may be worked up; then the resin little by little; after these the myrrh; then, when these are smooth, the remaining dry ingredients. When the days have been completed, make a cerate of the following sort: of wax 5 litrai; others, 240 drachms; of terebinth resin 1 litra; others, 30 drachms; of oil 2½ xestai; others, 6 kotylai; of galbanum 30 drachms; of liquid pitch 30 drachms. Add the galbanum last, then, having cooled it, take it up carefully. This, then, is the composition of the drug.

 [Plaster with squill.] Next after this one Criton wrote down another plaster of the same power in kind, in these very words: ℞ chalcitis, round birthwort, of each 30 dr. (others, 15 dr.); iris, 30 dr. (others, 15 dr.); dry pine resin, 30 dr. (others, 15 dr.); ammoniac, 15 dr.; myrrh, 15 dr.; storax, 15 dr.; flake of red copper, 15 dr.; roasted squill, 108 dr. Pound in the sharpest vinegar for 40 days, in the heats under the Dog-star. Add the galbanum last; then, having cooled it, take it up carefully. This, then, is the composition of the drug.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑποφοράν

Concerning its use, Criton  writes the following, in these very words: "In use, employ the drug undiluted for fractures in the head, for fistulas, for spreading sores (nomai), for excrescences of flesh, for the widening of orifices, and for undermining (hypophora). But if you wish to make it milder, so that it becomes flesh-producing and cicatrizing, to one part of the drug add twelve parts of rose cerate. In the case of burns, add eight to one part." These things Criton wrote well concerning the use of the drug, inasmuch as he used it, being pungent and opening and dispersing and on this account also drying, for fistulas and excrescences of flesh and for putrefactions, that is spreading sores, and for the widening of orifices, as is the case with sinuses that have narrow mouths; and for fractures in the head, whenever we attempt to treat them without trepanation—such being those that have not extended as far as the meninges; and for the sake of producing flesh, mixing in fourteen parts of rose cerate, until cicatrization. But it is better to say: until the filling up of the hollow in the ulcers.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὑποφοράν, ιδ΄ (14 parts, against ιβ΄ = 12 in Criton's quoted text; possibly a numeral corruption)

Concerning its use Criton writes the following  word for word: 'In use, employ the drug unmixed for fractures in the head, for fistulas, spreading ulcers (nomai) and fleshy excrescences, for widening openings and for drainage (hypophora). When you wish to make it milder, so that it becomes flesh-producing and cicatrising, add 12 parts of rose-oil cerate to one part of the drug. For burns, add 8 parts to one part.' Criton wrote well about the use of the drug. He used it as acrid, as opening (anastomotic), as discutient and for that reason also as drying, for fistulas, for fleshy excrescences and for putrefactions, that is, spreading ulcers, and for widening openings, as is the case with narrow-mouthed sinuses. He used it also for fractures in the head, when we attempt to treat them without trepanation; such fractures are those that have not penetrated as far as the meninges. For the sake of producing flesh he mixed in 14 parts of the rose-oil cerate, 'until cicatrisation'; but it would be better to say 'until the filling up of the cavity in the ulcers'.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐξ ἀνάγκης γὰρ τὰ νομώδη τῶν ἑλκῶν, ὅπερ ἔφην εἶναι ταὐτὸν ἐν τῷ σηπεδονώδει, θεραπευόμενα πρὸς τῶν ἰσχυρῶν  φαρμάκων ἐστὶν, ὁποῖον καὶ τὸ προκείμενον, πρῶτον μὲν ἀποῤῥύπτει τὰ σηπόμενα μόρια καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο γίνεται κοῖλα. μετὰ δὲ τὸ παύσασθαι τὴν σηπεδόνα καθάρσεώς τε καὶ πληρώσεως δεῖται, κᾀπειδὰν πληρωθῇ, τηνικαῦτα καὶ τῶν ἐπουλούντων. αὐτὸ δὲ τὸ φάρμακον, ὅταν μήπω κηρωτῇ μιχθῇ, δριμὺ τυγχάνον, οὐ μόνον ἀδύνατόν ἐστι σαρκοῦν, ἀλλὰ καὶ καθαίρει τὰ ὑπερσαρκοῦντα, καθάπερ αὐτὸς ὁ Κρίτων ἔφη. καὶ τούτου γε μάλιστα μεμνῆσθαι προσήκει λελεγμένου καὶ κατὰ τὴν πρὸ ταύτης ἔμπλαστρον, ἣν διὰ τῶν βοτανῶν ὠνόμασεν, ὅτι πάντα τὰ ἐκ τῶν θερμαινόντων καὶ τῶν ῥυπτικῶν συγκείμενα, κᾂν ἰσχυρῶς ᾖ δριμέα, κηρωτῇ μιγνύμενα σαρκωτικὰ γίνεται. τινὲς δὲ ἀπογινώσκουσιν αὐτῶν, ὅταν ἤτοι διπλασία τὴν κηρωτὴν μίξαντες ὁρῶσιν ἔτι δάκνουσαν οὕτως, ὡς μὴ δύνασθαι σαρκοῦν, ὥσπερ ἐπὶ ταύτης ἔχει τῆς ἐμπλάστρου. πολὺ γὰρ οὖσα δριμυτέρα τε καὶ δακνωδεστέρα τῆς διὰ βοτανῶν ἄχρηστος εἰς τὰς σαρκώσεις ἐστὶ, κᾂν τετραπλάσιον μιχθῇ τῇ κηρωτῇ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: θεραπευόμενα πρὸς τῶν ἰσχυρῶν φαρμάκων ἐστὶν, ἤτοι διπλασία

For of necessity, in the case of the spreading (eating) ulcers—which I said are the same thing as the putrefactive kind—when they are being treated by the strong  drugs, such as the one before us, first the drug cleans away the putrefying parts, and on this account they become hollow. Then, after the putrefaction has ceased, the ulcer requires both cleansing and filling up, and when it has been filled up, at that point also the cicatrizing agents. But the drug itself, when it has not yet been mixed with cerate, being acrid, is not only unable to produce flesh but also clears away the excrescent flesh, just as Criton himself said. And it is especially fitting to bear in mind this point, which was also stated in connection with the plaster before this one, which he named the plaster through herbs: that all compounds composed of heating and detergent substances, even if they are strongly acrid, become flesh-producing when mixed with cerate. Some, however, despair of them when, having mixed in the cerate in double quantity, they see it still biting to such a degree that it is unable to produce flesh, as is the case with this plaster. For being much more acrid and more biting than the plaster through herbs, it is useless for the production of flesh even if it is mixed with four times as much cerate.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐστὶν (θεραπευόμενα πρὸς τῶν ἰσχυρῶν φαρμάκων ἐστὶν: the syntax is broken, and I have construed the strong drugs as the subject of ἀποῤῥύπτει), ἐν τῷ σηπεδονώδει (rendered 'the same thing as the putrefying kind'), ἤτοι διπλασία (ἤτοι has no second alternative, so the text may be lacunose; διπλασία for expected διπλασίαν), ὑπερσαρκοῦντα

For of necessity the spreading (nomōdē) ulcers, which I said is the same thing as the putrefying kind, when they are treated by strong  drugs, such as the present one is, first have the putrefying parts cleansed away, and in this way they become hollow. After the putrefaction has ceased they need cleansing and filling, and once they have been filled, then also the cicatrizing drugs. The drug itself, when it has not yet been mixed with cerate, being acrid, is not only unable to produce flesh but also cleans away the overgrowing flesh, just as Criton himself said. And it is especially fitting to remember this point, which was also stated in connection with the plaster before this one, the one he named 'from herbs': that all compounds made up of heating and detergent ingredients, even if they are strongly acrid, become flesh-producing when mixed with cerate. Some, however, give up on them when, having mixed in either a double quantity of cerate, they see the plaster still biting so much that it cannot produce flesh, as is the case with this plaster. For being much more acrid and more biting than the one from herbs, it is useless for producing flesh, even if it is mixed with four times as much cerate.

συνετοῦ μὲν τοίνυν ἐστὶ χρεία καὶ τετριμμένου περὶ τὴν τῶν  τοιούτων φαρμάκων χρῆσιν, ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν εὐθέως ἐπινοοῦντος ποσαπλὰ δεῖται μίγνυσθαι τῇ κηρωτῇ, μετὰ δὲ τὴν πρώτην χρῆσιν ἔτι δριμυτέρου τοῦ φαρμάκου φανέντος ἢ ὥστε σαρκοῦν δύνασθαι, στοχάσασθαι πόσον ἔτι προσθεῖναι δεῖ τῆς κηρωτῆς, ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τῆς προκειμένης ὁ Κρίτων ἐποίησε. μὴ γάρ τοι δόκει κατὰ πρῶτον εὐθέως στοχασμὸν εὑρεῖν αὐτὸν ὡς χρὴ δωδεκαπλάσιον μίγνυσθαι τὴν κηρωτὴν, ἀλλ’ ὅτι πλέονα μὲν τῆς μιγνυμένης τῇ διὰ τῶν βοτανῶν ἔγνω, τὸ δ’ ὅσον πλέονα μετὰ τὸν πρῶτον στοχασμὸν εὗρεν. εἰ γὰρ ὀκταπλασίου μιχθείσης ἔτι δακνῶδες ἐφαίνετο, δεκαπλασίως ἐνέμιξεν, ὥσπερ γε εἰ καὶ δεκαπλάσιον, ἐπὶ τὴν δωδεκαπλάσιον ἧκεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

There is need, therefore, of an intelligent man and one practised in the use of  such drugs, who at the very outset considers how many times over it needs to be mixed with the cerate, and who, after the first use, if the drug still appears sharper than to be able to produce flesh, conjectures how much more of the cerate must still be added, as Criton did in the case of the present one. For do not suppose that he discovered straightaway, at the first conjecture, that the cerate must be mixed in twelvefold; rather, he recognised that it must be more than what is mixed with the plaster made of herbs, but how much more he found out after the first conjecture. For if, when eightfold had been mixed in, it still appeared biting, he mixed it in tenfold; and just so, if it was still biting at tenfold, he went on to the twelvefold.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ποσαπλὰ, δεκαπλασίως, εἰ καὶ δεκαπλάσιον

There is need, then, of an intelligent man, practised in the use of such  drugs, one who considers straightaway at the outset how many times its amount of cerate the drug needs to be mixed with, and who, if after the first application the drug still appears too pungent to be able to make flesh grow, estimates how much more of the cerate must still be added, just as Criton did with the plaster before us. For do not suppose that he found at his very first estimation that the cerate must be mixed in twelvefold; rather, he knew that more was needed than what is mixed with the plaster of herbs, but how much more he found after the first estimation. For if, when eightfold had been mixed in, it still appeared biting, he mixed it in tenfold, just as, if it was still so at tenfold too, he went on to twelvefold.

Greek (Kühn)

ἐπεὶ δ’, ὡς ἔφην, παρά τε τὸ πλῆθος τοῦ ῥύπου καὶ τὴν ὑγρότητα τοῦ ἕλκους τό τε εὐαίσθητον καὶ δυσαίσθητον τῶν συμπασχόντων, ἔτι τε τὴν μαλακότητα καὶ τὴν σκληρότητα τῆς τοὐ σώματος ἕξεως, ὑπαλλάττεσθαι χρὴ τὴν ποσότητα τῆς μιγνυμένης κηρωτῆς, ὅπερ εἴρηται πολλάκις, ἐννοεῖν ἡμᾶς δεῖ μεμνημένους τὴν ποσότητα τῆς χρήσεως ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις γράφεσθαι, πᾶσιν εὐπετοῦς ἐφ’ ἑκάτερα  τῆς ἐντεῦθεν ὁδοῦ πρὸς τὰ ἄλλα γιγνομένης ἄχρι τῶν ἄκρων. ἄκρα δὲ λέγω τὰ πρὸς ἁπάντων τῶν ὁμογενῶν σκοπῶν συμπληρώματα, περὶ ὧν ἓν εἰπόντος μου παράδειγμα νῦν ὑμᾶς χρὴ διὰ παντὸς αὐτοῦ μεμνημένους, μηκέτι ἀξιοῦν πολλάκις ἀκούειν ὑπὲρ αὐτοῦ. φημὶ δὴ σκοποὺς ὁμογενεῖς εἶναι τοῦ μὲν πλείστην μίγνυσθαι τὴν κηρωτὴν τό τε καθαρὸν εἷναι καὶ ξηρὸν τὸ ἕλκος, εὐαίσθητόν τε τὸ τοῦ θεραπευομένου σῶμα καὶ μαλακόν. τοῦ δὲ ἐλαχίστην ὑγρότητά τε πολλὴν κατὰ τὸ ἕλκος καὶ ῥύπον καὶ σκληρὸν καὶ δυσαίσθητον τὸ τοῦ κάμνοντος σῶμα. καλῶς μέντοι πᾶσι τοῖς συντεθεῖσιν αὐτὸ διαφορητικοῖς οὖσιν ἡ τοῦ χαλκοῦ λεπὶς ἐμίχθη ξηραντικὸν οὖσα φάρμακον, ἔχουσα δ’ οὐκ ὀλίγον ἐν αὐτῇ στύψεως. οὐ μόνον δὲ ἐπὶ ταύτης τῆς ἐμπλάστρου γινώσκειν ὑμᾶς χρὴ διὰ μίξεως πολλῆς κηρωτῆς σαρκωτικήν τε τὴν ἔμπλαστρον καὶ ἐπουλωτικὴν τῶν μὴ κακοήθων ἑλκῶν γινομένην, ἀλλὰ κᾀπὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπασῶν, ὅσαι τῆς αὐτῆς εἰσι δυνάμεως, ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐταῖς ἔγραψεν ὁ Κρίτων, τήν γε δι’ ἀκόνης ᾗ χρῆσθαί φησιν αὐτὸς καὶ οὕτως ἐκέλευσε συντιθέναι.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: συμπληρώματα, τὴν ποσότητα τῆς χρήσεως ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις γράφεσθαι, δι’ ἀκόνης

But since, as I said, in accordance with the amount of filth and the moistness of the ulcer, and the sensitivity or insensitivity of the parts that share in the affection, and further the softness and hardness of the bodily habit, the quantity of the cerate that is mixed in must be varied—which has been said many times—we must understand, keeping this in mind, that the quantity for use in such cases is written down with the road from there toward the other cases becoming easy for everyone in either direction  as far as the extremes. By extremes I mean the complete states formed from all the indications of the same kind; concerning which, once I have now stated a single example, you must keep it in mind throughout and no longer expect to hear about it repeatedly. I say, then, that the indications of the same kind for mixing in the greatest amount of cerate are the ulcer's being clean and dry, and the body of the person being treated being sensitive and soft; those for the least are much moisture and filth in the ulcer, and the body of the patient being hard and insensitive. Rightly, moreover, scale of copper was mixed with all the ingredients from which it is composed, these being discutient, since it is a drying drug and has in it no small amount of astringency. And it is not only in the case of this plaster that you must know that, through the admixture of much cerate, the plaster becomes flesh-producing and cicatrizing for ulcers that are not malignant, but also in the case of all the others that are of the same power; and next after them Criton wrote the one "with whetstone" (δι’ ἀκόνης), which he says he himself uses, and he directed that it be compounded thus.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τοὐ (presumably for τοῦ), τὴν ποσότητα τῆς χρήσεως ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις γράφεσθαι, τῆς ἐντεῦθεν ὁδοῦ, συμπληρώματα, ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐταῖς ἔγραψεν, δι’ ἀκόνης

Since, as I said, the quantity of cerate mixed in must be varied according to the amount of the filth and the moistness of the ulcer, the sensitivity or insensitivity of the parts that suffer along with it, and further the softness and the hardness of the habit of the body, as has been said many times, we must bear in mind and consider that in such recipes it is the quantity used that is written down, since for everyone the way from there towards the other cases in either  direction is easy, as far as the extremes. By extremes I mean the full concurrences of all the indications of the same kind. Concerning these I am now giving one example, and you must remember it always and no longer expect to hear about it many times. I say, then, that the indications of the same kind for mixing in the greatest amount of cerate are that the ulcer is clean and dry, and that the body of the patient being treated is sensitive and soft. The indications for mixing in the least are much moisture and filth in the ulcer, and a hard and insensitive body in the patient. It was, however, well done that copper flake was mixed with all the ingredients composing it, which are discutient, since it is a drying drug and has no little astringency in it. Not only in the case of this plaster must you recognise that the plaster becomes flesh-producing and cicatrizing for ulcers that are not malignant through the admixture of much cerate. The same holds for all the others that are of the same power, which Criton wrote down next after them, for instance the one made with whetstone, which he says he himself uses and which he directed to be compounded as follows.

 [Ἡ δι’ ἀκόνης.] Ἡ δι’ ἀκόνης ἐν χρήσει ἡμῖν οὖσα ποιεῖ πρὸς τὰ χρόνια πάντα καὶ ἰδίως πρὸς κεφαλαλγίαν ἐπὶ τὸ μέτωπον ἐπιτιθεμένη καὶ πρὸς πλευροῦ ἀλγήματα χρόνια, πρὸς ποδαγρικοὺς, ἰσχιαδικοὺς, ὤμων πόνους, ὀδονταλγίας, τοῖς τετριμμένοις μέρεσιν ἐντιθεμένη. κατασπᾷ καὶ ἔμμηνα ἐπάνω τοῦ κτενὸς ἐπιτιθεμένη. βοηθεῖ δὲ καὶ τοῖς τὸν στόμαχον ἀλγοῦσι καὶ ῥευματιζομένοις καὶ τροφὴν μὴ κρατοῦσιν, ἔστι δὲ καὶ πάγχρηστος χωρὶς κατακαύματος καὶ τῶν μὴ ἀνεχομένων δριμέος φαρμάκου· ἁρμόζει καὶ πρὸς σκληρίαν ἥπατος καὶ σπληνὸς καὶ πρὸς χοιράδας καὶ πρὸς ἰσχιαδικούς. ἰδίως δὴ χρῆσις ἐπ’ αὐτῶν γιγνέσθω ἡμέραις ἑπτά. ἐὰν δὲ τρυφεροὶ ὦσι, πέντε, ὕδατος τὸ μέρος ἢ ἐλαίου μὴ ἁπτέσθω, ἕως οὗ ψυδρακώσῃ καὶ ἰχῶρας ἐπισπάσηται παχεῖς, εἶτα λύσας τῇ λευκῇ κηρωτῇ δι’ ὕδατος χρῶ, ἐὰν δὲ πρακτικώτερον ἐθέλῃς, τῇ διὰ πεπέρεως ἀκόνῃ. ♃ κηροῦ, χαλβάνης, τερμινθίνης, πιτυΐνης φρυκτῆς, ἐλαίου δαφνίνου, ἐλαίου παλαιοῦ ἀνὰ λίτραν α΄. λεπίδος χαλκοῦ λίτραν α΄ S".

 [The plaster with whetstone.] The plaster with whetstone (ἀκόνη), which is in use among us, is effective against all chronic conditions, and specifically against headache when applied to the forehead, and against chronic pains of the side; for those with gout, those with sciatica, pains of the shoulders, toothaches, when it is put onto the worn parts (τετριμμένοις). It also draws down the menses when applied above the pubes. It helps also those who have pain in the stomach-mouth and those who suffer from flux and those who do not retain food; and it is useful for everything, apart from a burn and from those who do not tolerate an acrid drug. It is suited also to hardness of the liver and of the spleen, and to scrofulous swellings, and to those with sciatica. In their case, specifically, let the use be for seven days; but if they are delicate, five; let the part not touch water or oil until it has raised pustules and drawn out thick ichors; then, having removed it, use the white cerate made with water; but if you want something more active, the whetstone-plaster with pepper. Take: wax, galbanum, terebinth, roasted pine resin, laurel oil, old oil, one litra of each; copper scale, one and a half litrae.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χωρὶς κατακαύματος καὶ τῶν μὴ ἀνεχομένων, τοῖς τετριμμένοις μέρεσιν ἐντιθεμένη, ψυδρακώσῃ, ῥευματιζομένοις, ♃, S"

 [The plaster made with whetstone.] The plaster made with whetstone, which is in use among us, is effective against all chronic conditions, and specifically against headache when applied to the forehead, and against chronic pains of the side, for gouty patients, sciatic patients, pains of the shoulders, and toothaches, when placed on the parts after they have been rubbed. It also draws down the menses when applied above the pubes. It also helps those who have pain in the stomach, those who suffer from flux, and those who do not retain their food. It is also all-purpose, being without burning, even for those who do not tolerate an acrid drug. It is suitable also for hardness of the liver and spleen, for scrofulous swellings, and for sciatic patients. In their case specifically, let the application be made for seven days, or, if they are delicate, for five. Let the part not be touched by water or oil until it breaks out in small blisters and draws out thick ichors. Then, having taken it off, use the white cerate made with water, or, if you want something more effective, the whetstone plaster made with pepper. ♃ Wax, galbanum, terebinth, roasted pine resin, laurel oil, old oil, of each 1 litra; flake of copper 1½ litrae.

 of verdigris, 6 ounces; of ammoniac incense, 1 ounce; of mustard, adarkē, sulphur, 1 ounce each; of southernwood, centaury, chalcanthum, salt ammoniac, frankincense, common salt, round alum, litharge, propolis, Cnidian berry, 3 ounces each. Having pounded and sifted them all together, take them up in the astringents (στυπτικοῖς) together with the oils. This, then, is the composition of the plaster before us, which differs not a little from the one written out before in heat. At any rate it contains mustard and adarkē, sulphur and Cnidian berry, which reach such a degree of heat as to ulcerate whatever they remain in contact with for too long. And laurel oil too is among the things that heat considerably, and after these chalcanthum and the salts. You have learned, moreover, that these also partake of astringency, the salts of a slight one, chalcanthum of a great deal. But concerning the mixing of astringent drugs with the discutient ones it has been stated previously, in the case of the plasters of many uses (polychrēsta). For in general nothing can become a polychreston (of many uses) unless it also partakes of the opposite faculty.

 of verdigris, 6 oz.; of ammoniac incense, 1 oz.; of mustard, adarce and sulphur, 1 oz. each; of southernwood, centaury, chalcanthum, ammoniac salt, frankincense, common salt, round alum, litharge, propolis and Cnidian berry, 3 oz. each. Greek (Kühn)

ἄν τε γὰρ ἁπλῶς διαφορητικὴ δύναμις ᾖ μόνη, πρὸς ἓν εἶδος ἁρμόσει παθῶν, ὃ διαφορεῖσθαι προσῆκεν, ἄν τε  στυπτικῆς, πρὸς ἓν ἕτερον, ἐφ’ οὗ τὸ ἐπιῤῥέον ἀναστέλλειν προσήκει. διὸ τὰ μὲν στύφοντα τοῖς ἀρχομένοις ἐξ ἐπιῤῥοῆς ὑγρῶν γεννᾶσθαι πάθεσιν ἁρμόττει. τὰ δὲ διαφοροῦντα χωρὶς τῆς τῶν στυφόντων μίξεως, ἐφ’ ὧν σωμάτων οὐδὲν ἐπιῤῥυῆναι δύναται διὰ τὸ προκεκενῶσθαι, ὡς ἐάν γε περιουσία τις ᾖ χυμῶν, οὐ μόνον διαφορεῖται τὸ περιεχόμενον ὑγρὸν ἐν τοῖς πεπονθόσιν, ἀλλὰ καὶ παραγίνεται πρὸς αὐτὰ τῶν διαφορούντων ἕλκειν πεφυκότων ὑφ’ ἑαυτὰ, καθάπερ τὰ περιεχόμενα ψαύοντα τῶν ὑγρῶν, οὕτως καὶ τὰ κατὰ βάθος ὄντα τοῦ σώματος. εὐλόγως δὴ μίγνυται τοῖς διαφορητικοῖς, τά τε σταλτικὰ καὶ ἀποκρουστικὰ προσαγορευόμενα, τοῦ μὲν ἐπὶ τὴν σύνθεσιν αὐτῶν ἀφικομένου τῷ λογισμῷ τούτῳ χρησαμένου, τῆς πείρας δὲ μαρτυρούσης τῷ λογισμῷ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στυπτικῆς (genitive without expressed noun), καθάπερ τὰ περιεχόμενα ψαύοντα τῶν ὑγρῶν (syntax of the comparison), σταλτικὰ

For if the power is simply dispersive alone, it will suit one single kind of affections, that which ought to be dispersed; and if  it is of an astringent kind, one other, in which it is fitting to drive back that which flows in. Hence the astringent drugs suit those affections which are beginning to be generated from an influx of humours, whereas the dispersive drugs, without the admixture of astringents, suit those bodies into which nothing can flow because they have been previously evacuated; since, if there is some superfluity of humours, not only is the fluid contained in the affected parts dispersed, but fluid also arrives at them, because the dispersive drugs are naturally such as to draw under themselves, just as they draw the fluids contained in the parts they touch, so also those lying in the depth of the body. Reasonably, then, there are mixed with the dispersive drugs those called checking and repellent, the man who arrived at their composition having used this reasoning, and experience bearing witness to the reasoning.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: στυπτικῆς (genitive after ἄν τε, where nominative στυπτική would be expected; possibly corrupt), καθάπερ τὰ περιεχόμενα ψαύοντα τῶν ὑγρῶν (construction unclear; perhaps τὰ περιέχοντα), παραγίνεται (subject understood; supplied as 'more fluid'), σταλτικὰ

For if the faculty is simply discutient and nothing else, it will suit one kind of affection, the kind that ought to be dispersed; and if  astringent, it will suit one other kind, in which it is proper to check what flows in. For this reason astringents suit affections that are beginning to arise from an influx of fluids, while discutients without the admixture of astringents suit those bodies into which nothing can flow because they have been evacuated beforehand. For if there is some superabundance of humours, not only is the fluid contained in the affected parts dispersed, but more fluid also comes to them, since discutients naturally draw to themselves not only those of the fluids that lie around and in contact, but also those that are in the depth of the body. With good reason, then, the drugs called checking and repellent are mixed with the discutients: the man who arrived at their composition used this reasoning, and experience bears witness to the reasoning.

εἰκότως οὖν κατὰ τὸ προκείμενον φάρμακον, καίτοι σκοπὸν ἔχοντος θερμῆναί τε καὶ ξηρᾶναι καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ ψύδρακας ποιεῖν, ἐμίχθη τι καὶ τῶν στυφόντων, ἀλλ’ οὐχ ἱκανά γε πρὸς πολλὰ θερμαίνοντα σφοδρῶς φάρμακα, δύο ταῦτα χάλκανθος καὶ ἅλες, ἀλλὰ καὶ λεπὶς χαλκοῦ κατὰ τὸν αὐτὸν  τὸν ἐμίχθη λογισμὸν ἥ τε στυπτηρία, διὰ τὸ σφοδρῶς στύφειν καὶ τοὔνομα λαβοῦσα τοῦτο. ἄλλη. ἄλλην ἔμπλαστρον ἔγραψεν ὁ Κρίτων ἐνδοξοτάτην, ἣν ὀνομάζει Μεσιανὴν, ἕτερον ὕλης ἔχουσα γένος τῆς προγεγραμμένης καὶ τῆς μελλούσης ὑπ’ ἐμοῦ γραφήσεσθαι νῦν ἐφεξῆς, ἣν αὐτὸς ὁ Κρίτων ἀνίκητον ὀνομάζει, συγκειμένην ἐκ δριμέων τῶν πλείστων φαρμάκων, οὐκ οὔσης τῆς Μεσιανῆς τοιαύτης, ὡς μετὰ ταῦτα δειχθήσεται κατὰ τὸν οἰκεῖον αὐτῆς καιρόν. καὶ μέντοι καὶ κεφαλικὰς ἄλλας ὀνομάζων, ὧν ἡ καθόλου δύναμις ὀλίγον ὕστερον εἰρήσεται, γράψας ἐφεξῆς τῇ Μεσιανῇ τὴν καλουμένην, μετὰ ταῦτα ἀνίκητον ὑφ’ ἑαυτοῦ γράφει, παραπλησίας δυνάμεως οὖσαν τῇ προγεγραμμένῃ, ἣν δι’ ἀκόνης ἐκάλει. ἔχει δὲ ἡ περὶ αὐτῆς λέξις τοῦ Κρίτωνος ὧδέ πως αὐτοῖς ὀνόμασιν. ἡ ἀνίκητος ἐν χρήσει ἡμῖν οὖσα. ἔστι δὲ πάγχρηστος, ἐπισπᾶται, ῥήσσει, ἀνακαθαίρει, κολλᾷ, ἐξιποῖ, διὰ σπληνίου καὶ παραθέσεως σωλήνων καὶ ἀνιεμένη ἔχουσα πάντοτε πηλὸν δι’ ὀξελαίου.

Reasonably, then, in the case of the drug before us, although it has as its aim to heat and to dry, and for this reason also to produce psydrakes (pustules), something of the astringents too was mixed in — though not ones sufficient in relation to the many strongly heating drugs, these two, chalcanthum and salts; but scale of copper too was mixed in according to the same  reasoning, and alum, which has received this very name on account of its astringing strongly. Another. Crito wrote another most renowned plaster, which he calls the Mesiane, having a different kind of material from the one written above and from the one about to be written by me now next in order, which Crito himself calls 'Invincible', being composed of drugs most of which are acrid, whereas the Mesiane is not of this sort, as will be shown after this at its own proper occasion. And indeed, also naming others 'cephalic', whose general power will be stated a little later, having written them next after the one called Mesiane, after these he writes the one called 'Invincible' by himself, which is of a power similar to the one written above, which he called the 'by means of the whetstone'. Crito's text concerning it runs roughly as follows, in these very words: The Invincible, which is in use with us. It is useful for everything: it draws, it breaks open, it cleanses, it glues together, it presses out (ἐξιποῖ), by means of a compress and the placing-alongside of channels (σωλήνων), and also when dissolved, always having a clay-paste (πηλὸν) made with vinegar-and-oil.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ψύδρακας, τὸν τὸν (dittography), γράψας ἐφεξῆς τῇ Μεσιανῇ τὴν καλουμένην, μετὰ ταῦτα ἀνίκητον ὑφ’ ἑαυτοῦ γράφει, δι’ ἀκόνης, ἐξιποῖ, σωλήνων, ἀνιεμένη ἔχουσα πάντοτε πηλὸν δι’ ὀξελαίου

But since these two, chalcanthum and salt, are not sufficient against many violently heating drugs, copper flake too was mixed in by the same  reasoning, and so was alum, which also took this very name because it astringes violently. Another. Crito recorded another, very celebrated plaster, which he names the Mesian. Its material is of a different kind from that of the plaster written above, and from that of the one I am about to write next, which Crito himself names the invincible and which is composed mostly of acrid drugs. The Mesian is not of this sort, as will be shown later at its own proper occasion. Moreover, he also names certain others 'cephalic', whose general property will be stated a little later. Having written these next after the Mesian, he afterwards writes the one he himself calls the invincible, which is of a property closely similar to the one written above, the one he called 'the whetstone plaster'. Crito's text about it runs roughly thus, in these very words: 'The invincible, which is in use with us. It is all-purpose: it draws out, it ruptures, it cleanses out, it conglutinates, it squeezes out, by means of a compress and the application of tubes; and when it is dissolved it always has the consistency of mud, with oxelaeum.'

It also disperses indurations and helps contractions of the sinews, being applied without  fomentation, so that they are not chilled all round. And when loosened it serves in the role of an unguent, like an acopon; for it also stops the fatigues that occur at the beginnings of diseases and those arising from an unclear cause. It is also a malagma for the extremities, applied in large compresses. It is also agglutinative of fresh bleeding wounds, once they have been sutured or fastened with clasps, a folded pad of vinegar alone being applied in addition over the compress, cold in summer, warm in winter. It is effective also against the affections in the soles of the feet and against cuts, putrefactions, conditions around the fingers and toes, and fractures accompanied by wounds. It is possible to use neither any other flesh-producing nor cicatrizing remedy, but to trust in it alone. It is effective also against human bites, dog bites, and bites of wild beasts. Its greatest profession is that it does not allow concealed abscesses to form in the colon and the peritoneum, provided at least that suppuration has not already taken place, or, when they have formed, it thins them and makes them burst into the intestines. One must apply a compress to the place, and on top of it a felt pad of warm oxelaion (oil and vinegar), and foment twice a day.

The unmixed resin-wax is one part wax, three parts roasted resin, melted together with a little oil; the medium one  is one part wax, two parts resin; the relaxed and well-tempered one is one part wax, one part resin; the slackened one is two parts wax, one part resin; the over-relaxed one is three parts wax, one part resin. It is also recorded that, in the case of wild lichen, it has proved suitable when applied unmixed. These things Crito wrote in these very words. I, for my part, shall make the beginning of my discussion from the last of the things said by him. They call lichens 'wild' when they see them receiving no benefit from the weakly drying drugs, but being irritated by the strong ones. In their case, therefore, there is need of a drug that is sufficiently active, yet not violently biting, which, as I said, having been composed on the basis of skilled conjecture, is put to the test by experience. The drug before us is composed of many acrid, fine-particled and discutient substances. It also contains some of those that cleanse without biting and some of those that are slightly astringent.

The acrid ingredients among those in it, then, are pyrethrum and mustard and Cnidian berry and pigeon's dung, verdigris and bay-berry and nitron, then the so-called ammoniac salts  and, in addition to these, the acrid vinegar; for that is the kind he requires to be put in. For it was necessary, as was shown before, that this drug should also partake somewhat of the astringent faculty, and for this very reason scale was mixed in abundantly. And if the manna really is manna and not frankincense, it too would have something astringent. And it too has been put in much more abundantly than the scale, reasonably, so that it may soothe the vehemence of the acrid drugs, along with concocting the humours in the affected part. Most soothing and most concocting, together with dispersing in some degree, is the decoction of dried figs. All these, then, mixed together have reasonably made the drug a polychreston (of many uses).

And to their active efficacy the vinegar contributes  most of all, in which he orders the drugs to be ground fine under the summer sun. But the vinegar contributes most of all to their efficacy ; he directs that the drugs be ground in it under the summer sun. For all things prepared in this way become fine-particled, and for this reason their power sinks down into the depth of bodies. [On the plasters of the same kind written up by Heras.] The plaster made with herbs, whose preparation by Criton I wrote out above, Heras himself also prepares in the same way, as Criton too recorded of him. But he differs in the preparation with squill. For of the ammoniac, the myrrh, the pine resin, the storax, the flake and the squill, Criton mixes in with the other ingredients as much as Heras does. But in the case of the birthwort and the iris he differs. For whereas Heras wrote: round birthwort 30 dr., dry iris 30 dr. (others, 10), Criton in turn afterwards wrote as follows: ℞ birthwort 30 dr., iris 30 dr. (others, 40). At the end of the preparation Heras wrote: 'When the days have been completed, make a cerate as follows: ℞ wax 5 pounds (others, 40 dr.), terebinth 1 pound (others, 30 dr.).'

of ammoniac  incense 168 drachms, of Colophonian pine resin 68 drachms, of wax 168 drachms, of galbanum 20 drachms, of verdigris 50 drachms (others, 20 drachms), of myrrh 4 drachms (others, 8 drachms). Having melted each one of them attentively, so that it does not burn on, lift it off again and add the galbanum and the verdigris; then, setting it on the fire, boil until it becomes quince-yellow and of good consistency. Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τεθεραπευμένου, συστραφῇ

Having put the litharge and the verdigris, finely ground, into a pot and poured on three cyathi of oil, boil over a gentle fire, stirring with a wooden spatula, until it becomes green; then, having taken it off the fire and poured it into a mortar, grind it for a considerable time while it is hot.  Then again, having poured into the pot three cyathi of the oil, boil it, stirring so that it does not burn on, until it becomes quince-yellow, and again, having emptied it out, grind it as one does collyria. And again, having taken it up into the pot, boil it, stirring, until it becomes red, and having emptied it into a mortar again, for the third time, grind it for a considerable time; and again, for the fourth time, boil it in the pot so that it thickens together, and again, while it is hot, having poured it out and ground it, take it up and store it in a red box, and use it for the conditions mentioned. Next after the one written above, Andromachus wrote word for word as follows concerning another plaster. A moist plaster for spreading sores (nomai), which I use, and for many other things. Rendering A (fable-5-1)

for the works of the art there is need both of method and of practice] That of remedies  some are discovered by reason alone, some by experience, and this too without requiring reason, while certain ones require both working together with one another, has often been demonstrated to you; and in addition to this, that the things discovered through reason and experience make use of a conjectural road for the discovery of what is sought, and, having been hoped for by reason, are confirmed by experience; and moreover, that in conjectural hope there is no small difference in respect of the more and the less, just as also throughout the whole of life. For we do not hope alike that there will be rain at the winter solstice and at the rising of the Dog-star. For at the winter solstice it rarely fails to occur, whereas at the rising of the Dog-star it rarely occurs. But since all the works of the arts are brought to exactness by continuous practice, it is not sufficient to learn only the universal in them, but one must also act many times in the particular cases. For practice through imperfect activities is the road to the acquisition of perfect ones.

For this reason, then, I too, in all the treatises which I composed for you, my companions, at your request, did not content myself with the general theorems alone, but also added many exercises,  by means of which I hoped that your knowledge of them would become clearer and more secure. For in the commentaries on the power of simple drugs, which are eleven in number, I wrote up the powers in the particular materials, all of them, or at any rate the greatest number of the most useful ones.

For indeed this was shown to be necessary for a ready supply of remedies, and not only so that you yourselves too may be able, without prepared drugs, in situations of want to be well supplied with remedies—as Erasistratus once found a supply of the juice of herbs when he did not have a stomatic drug with him in that place on his journey—but also so that, even if you lose the written recipes of those who compounded them, you yourselves may nevertheless be able  to compound ones closely similar to those that were lost. For the general method was written by none of my predecessors, neither accurately with distinctions nor deficiently without distinctions; but neither did anyone write down a drug professing to cure those who have been pricked in a nerve by some sharp object—such as needles and children's styluses are—a drug able to do this one thing alone excellently, whereas I went through a not easily counted multitude of them in the aforementioned book.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κο.

Some of them, however, who wrote down notable drugs of many uses (polychrēsta) — those at least who were more careful — added, in addition to the other things they profess these drugs  to do, sometimes that they are beneficial also for those wounded in the nerves, at other times for severings of nerves and muscles, and there are times when they said that they also help punctures or contusions of them. But you, from what you have learned, are able to recognise before trial whether someone professes things possible or impossible concerning what he has written. And it is also possible for you, in the case of drugs that have been written down without a statement of what they are naturally able to do, to recognise the conditions on which they are to be used. So then, in order that I may begin, by way of example, with a certain plaster drug of many uses (polychrēston), written second in order in the pharmacological book of Andromachus which he composed on the drugs applied externally to the body, without his indicating for what it is especially to be used, it is possible for you once again to exercise yourselves in how one must recognise, from the simple drugs written down, the power of the compound. [The grey Egyptian of Andromachus.] For Andromachus wrote as follows, word for word: The grey Egyptian. Take litharge, 44 drachms; castor oil, 6 cotylae; wax, 144 drachms; ammoniac incense, 72 drachms; terebinth resin, 36 drachms.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: φαρμακωνῖτιν, κίκεως κο. στ΄

Some of them, however, having written up noteworthy polychrest drugs (those, at least, who were more careful), added, besides the other things which they profess  these do, sometimes that they are also beneficial to the nerve-wounded, at other times that they are so for severances of the nerves and muscles, and there are times when they said that they also help punctures or contusions of these. But you, from what you have learned, are able to recognise before trial whether someone professes possible or impossible things concerning what he has written. And you are able also, with drugs written down without a statement of what they are by nature able to do, to recognise in which cases they are to be used. So, to begin, by way of example, with a certain polychrest plaster drug, written second in order in Andromachus' pharmaceutical book (φαρμακωνῖτιν), which he composed on the remedies applied to the body from outside, without his having indicated for what it is chiefly to be used, you may again exercise yourselves in how one must recognise, from the simple drugs written down, the faculty of the compound. [The grey Egyptian of Andromachus.] For Andromachus wrote thus, word for word: The grey Egyptian. ♃ Of litharge, 44 drachms; of castor oil, 6 kotylai; of wax, 144 drachms; of ammoniac incense, 72 drachms; of terebinth resin, 36 drachms.

of burnt wool  18 drachms; of scale of steel (stomoma) 8 drachms; of scale of copper 8 drachms; of aristolochia 8 drachms; of frankincense 8 drachms; some add myrrh 4 drachms; of opopanax 2 drachms; of sea-water 50 drachms, for the soaking of the wax. This, then, is the recipe of the drug, which is in truth a good one and of many uses; but he himself omitted to say in which cases it is to be used. Now if, having first stated the power of each of the simple drugs, as we did in eleven books, and having also written some general method of the composition of drugs, as I did in the book preceding this one, he had proposed such recipes to the others then living for the sake of exercise, so that they might answer for which affections it can be useful, we would truly have praised his teaching. But since he did none of these things, one might find fault with him in every way. For this reason, then, his books on drugs, although they contain the compositions of a very great many excellent drugs, have with good reason been neglected by physicians.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

For among a thousand, some one person seems to possess the perfection of the art, while the rest,  just like laymen, are content if they are able to use correctly the drugs that have been clearly written up. And why need one speak of such physicians, when even some of those called Empiricists, although they have shown themselves noteworthy through the works of the art, nevertheless say that they do not know by what reasoning each of the compound drugs was discovered—and why do I say 'by what reasoning'? For they say that none of such things was discovered by reasoning at all. Useless to them, therefore, is the prescription of this grey plaster, which in its heading teaches only the colour, without the professed virtue in respect of its power, which it would have been better to have written before the colour. Since, then, Andromachus omitted to state its power, let us attempt to discover it from the kind and the power of the drugs that are mixed in it. Now you have learned that litharge, being of the middle mixture with respect to heating and cooling, has departed slightly towards drying.

Now as for litharge, you have learned that, while it is of a mean temperament as regards heating and cooling, it has departed a little toward drying.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κο. στ΄., ταὐτὸ ἦν ὄνομα τῆς κοτύλης

And indeed you also know that its use in plasters is twofold: if it remains unmelted, as material only; but if it is melted, also as something able to dry,  and the more so if it is boiled for longer. Now, however, there is a question as to how far it will be boiled, since Andromachus has written that six cotylae of kiki, which is castor oil, must be mixed in. For he did not make clear of how many ounces, reckoned from the Roman litra, he wants the cotyle to be; and yet it would have been better for one who had lived in Rome to speak of litrai and xestai and ounces, and to leave aside the name of the cotyle, which is in use in the Greek cities outside Italy. Now if the name of the cotyle were the same in all of them, there would be no question. But as it is, since there is a very great difference in quantity among them, he ought to have said either 'I mean the Attic cotyle' or the Alexandrian or the Ephesian or some other. Now the majority of those who have written on measures and weights say that the cotyle written of by physicians in the pharmacological books is of nine ounces, of those reckoned from the Roman litra. But others say that the one of twelve ounces is meant by them, just as in Rome they customarily name the litra of oil.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: κατατετμημένων κεράτων

But I have already heard someone saying that the kotyle written by the physicians in the pharmacological books  has sixteen Roman ounces; and it is no small difference with respect to the power of the drug whether one puts in large kotylai or small ones. I, at any rate, in Rome once weighed the so-called litra of oil, which they measure by means of the graduated horns, wishing to learn how much weight it has by the balance. And I found that the twelve measure-ounces of oil are equal to ten weight-ounces. Therefore the physicians ought to write more carefully in the pharmacological books what sort of ounces or litrai of the liquid drugs they order to be put in, whether the measure-ones or the weight-ones. For it is evident that of these liquid bodies, just as of the solid ones, some are heavier and others lighter. And the weight of each of the liquids put into drugs has been stated by me in another treatise; but now let us resume the matter before us. For as to the three kotylai, if one sets each at nine measure-ounces, he will make the three come to twenty-seven ounces.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τρεῖς μετρικὰς ἐλαίου μίξεις τρισὶ σταθμικαῖς τῆς λιθαργύρου, τῶν μετρικῶν οὐγγιῶν

For this reason some have mixed in either terebinth resin or the transparent kind called colophony, trying, as far as possible, to harm neither the colour nor the power of the drug,  and to add to it, by the admixture of the resin, the property of not easily falling off, because the drug becomes crumbly if so much of the litharge and of the white lead is boiled in so much oil. Others, on the same principle, added white wax to the drug once it had been boiled. To litharge that has double the quantity of oil, however, even if you add nothing resinous or waxy, the adhesive quality will belong as a result of the boiling, and much more so if you mix in triple the oil to the litharge, that is, if you mix three measure-litrai of oil with three weight-litrai of litharge. It is likely, then, that Andromachus wants the castor oil to be put in at nine metric ounces [per kotyle], so that the six kotylai come to fifty-four metric ounces, which is the same as four and a half Roman metric litrai. And it is quite clear that, if at some time castor oil is not available to us, we shall take old oil in its place.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: πλαστικωτέρα, αὐχμηρὸν

And I myself have often put Sicyonian oil into the discutient plasters, and when that too was not available, I improvised another oil and made it similar to those, by boiling down in it roots of wild cucumber and bryony  and marshmallow. That Andromachus wants the drug before us to be both discutient and emollient is quite evident from his putting castor oil into it, and a very large amount of ammoniac incense. For he wants the wax to be equal to the litharge in weight, and the ammoniac a half; and yet in many of the renowned plasters this drug is put in not at a half, but at a fourth part or a fifth, and often a seventh and an eighth, and sometimes a tenth, by weight, in relation to the litharge. Concerning wax we have learned that it holds the rank of material in the compositions of such drugs, not the contribution of an active power. It too possesses some slight emollient power, but it has not been taken up into the drug before us on account of this, just as the terebinth resin has not either, although it is far more plastic (πλαστικωτέρα) in power than wax. For indeed it softens more than wax does, and in addition it heats and disperses, whereas wax has neither of these. But the ammoniac incense, if put in alone, will make the drug dry and crumbly (αὐχμηρόν).

But for  plaster-making—for it is no worse to name it thus—both the wax and the resin are useful. For in fact softening and dispersing belong to the drug even from the ammoniac alone, which holds the first place among emollients and is a rival also to the discutients. And if you recall what was said in the fifth book of On the Powers of Simple Drugs, as to what sort of mixture such drugs must have, you will have no further need of fresh instruction. For the primary active powers of drugs lie in heat and cold and moisture and dryness; the secondary are the emollient, the discutient, the repellent, the attractive, the concocting, the detergent and the septic, just as the tertiary are those agglutinative of wounds, the cicatrizing and the flesh-producing. And so the drug compounded from castor oil and ammoniac incense is sufficient to disperse and to soften and, evidently, also to dry, having taken on as suitable material the wax and the resin and the litharge that has been boiled for a longer time.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: στομώματος

And he orders that there be added to it  also some burnt wool and aristolochia, drugs that are most discutient and most desiccative; after these he mentions a concocting and soothing drug, frankincense. And if we should also put in some myrrh and opopanax—for he says that some people mix some of these too into the drug—we know that myrrh heats and dries in no ignoble fashion, while opopanax does both of these even more vigorously and is moreover of an attenuating and discutient faculty. But of this only a very little is put in, since, I think, the one who added it feared the acridity of the drug and wished to make the plaster not only discutient and emollient but also suitable for wounds. For this reason, then, he also mixed into it the scale of copper and of steel (στομώματος), drugs that have an astringent faculty together with drying strongly. At any rate, on account of the admixture of these, the Egyptian plaster under discussion becomes a good drug for bleeding wounds and for not a few other ulcers.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

And indeed also for abscesses and for all  such things as need to be dispersed, you have often heard that those remedies which, together with the discutient drugs, contain also something of the astringents become safer, whereas those without the astringent ingredients are more effective, but not safe. For often such a remedy draws fluxes, when the body being treated is plethoric or full of bad humours. But if the body is empty and free of superfluity, the more discutient drugs, taken by themselves without the astringents, reach their goal more readily. Since, however, the drugs are applied to such bodies only in few cases, for this reason it has seemed safer to mix in also something of the repellents. Thus indeed some have added a little chalcanthum to the discutient plaster made with salts, although the salts themselves also have something astringent.

τὸ τοίνυν προκείμενον φάρμακον εὕρηται μὲν διαφορητικὸν ἰσχυρῶς, ὡς καὶ πῦον ἤδη τό γε μὴ πάνυ πολὺ, χωρὶς τοῦ διατρῆσαι τὸ δέρμα, διαφορεῖν δύνασθαι, μαλακτικὸν δὲ τῶν σκιῤῥουμένων τε καὶ ὁπωσοῦν σκληρυνομένων, κολλητικὸν δὲ μεγάλων τραυμάτων, ὥσπερ γε καὶ τῶν ἤδη κακοηθευομένων ἑλκῶν ἐπουλωτικὸν  ἁπάντων τε τῶν ἄλλων παθημάτων, ὅσα διαφορητικῆς τε ἅμα καὶ ἀποκρουστικῆς μετρεῖται δυνάμεως, ἐν οἷς ἐστι καὶ τὰ κατὰ τοῦ μετώπου ῥευματιζομένων ὀφθαλμῶν ἐπιτιθέμενα, καλούμενα δὲ ὑπὸ τῶν ἰατρῶν ἀνακολλήματα. ταὐτὸν δὲ τοῦτο τὸ φάρμακον ἐπιτήδειον ἔσται καὶ τοῖς γεγυμνωμένοις νεύροις, εἰ καὶ τετμημένα κατά τι μέρος αὐτῶν τύχοι. τὰ γάρ τοι διακεκομμένα τὸν ἐκ τῆς συμπαθείας οὐκ ἔχοντα κίν δυνον ἔτι καὶ τὴν αἴσθησιν ἀφαιρεῖται τῶν ὑποκειμένων ἑαυτῷ σωμάτων, εἰς ἃ κατίσχεται. δεῖταί γε μὴν καὶ ταῦτα τοιούτου φαρμάκου. τὰ γὰρ ὑγραίνοντα σήπει ῥᾳδίως αὐτὰ, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ οἱ ξηραντικοὶ κυκλίσκοι, καίτοι στύφοντες ἱκανῶς, ὅμως ἁρμόττουσι τοῖς γεγυμνωμένος νεύροις, ἅ γε μήπω φθάσαντα πρὸς τὴν ἀκμὴν τῆς φλεγμονῆς ἀφίκετο. ταῦτα γὰρ ἀγανακτεῖ καὶ παροξύνεται πρὸς τῶν τοιούτων φαρμάκων. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τούτων ἐπὶ προήκοντι τῷ λόγῳ ῥηθήσεται· περὶ δὲ τῆς προκειμένης ἐμπλάστρου τὰ λείποντα τῷ λόγῳ προσθῶμεν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: μετρεῖται, ἑαυτῷ, κατίσχεται

The drug under discussion, then, has been found to be strongly discutient, so that it is able to disperse even pus already present—at least when it is not very abundant—without perforating the skin; emollient for parts that are becoming scirrhous or hardened in any way whatever; agglutinative of large wounds, just as it is also cicatrizing of ulcers that are already becoming malignant,  and of all the other affections that are apportioned (μετρεῖται) a power at once discutient and repellent, among which are also the things applied to the forehead for eyes affected by flux, which are called by physicians anakollēmata. This same drug will also be suitable for nerves that have been laid bare, even if they happen to have been cut in some part of them. For those that have been cut through, no longer having the danger that arises from sympathy, also take away the sensation of the bodies lying beneath it (ἑαυτῷ), into which it is held down (κατίσχεται). Yet these too require such a drug. For moistening agents easily putrefy them; for this reason the drying small disks too, although they are considerably astringent, are nevertheless suitable for nerves that have been laid bare—those, at least, which have not yet already arrived at the peak of inflammation. For these are irritated and exacerbated by such drugs. But these matters will be spoken of as the discussion proceeds; concerning the plaster under discussion, let us add what is lacking to the account.

The drug before us, then, has been found to be powerfully discutient, so that it is able to disperse even pus already formed, at least when it is not very much, without perforating the skin; and to be emollient of parts that are becoming scirrhous and of those hardened in any way whatever; and agglutinative of large wounds, just as it is also cicatrizing of ulcers that have already turned malignant,  and of all the other affections that are measured by (? μετρεῖται) a faculty at once discutient and repellent.
