This same drug will also be suitable for nerves that have been laid bare, even if they happen to have been cut in some part of them. For nerves that have been cut right through no longer carry the danger that arises from sympathy, and they also take away sensation from the bodies lying beneath them, into which they are distributed (? κατίσχεται). Yet these too need a drug of this kind. For moistening drugs readily putrefy them. For this reason the drying little discs too, although they are considerably astringent, nevertheless suit nerves that have been laid bare, at least those that have not yet arrived at the peak of the inflammation. ἐπεὶ δὲ οἱ πολλοὶ τῶν ἰατρῶν ἀγνοοῦντες ἐπιτιθέασι τὰς τοιαύτας· ἐμπλάστρους σκληρὰς, ἄμεινον ἐμμότους ποιῆσαι τήξαντας  ἢ κατὰ τὴν σκευασίαν εὐθὺς ἐργάσασθαι μαλθακάς. ἔπειτα δὲ ὅταν ἐπὶ μεγάλων διαιρέσεων, ἐν αἷς γεγύμνωται νεῦρα, πειραθῶσιν αὐτῶν, ἐλπίζουσιν ἐπὶ τῶν νυγμάτων ἔσεσθαι πολὺ μᾶλλον ὠφελίμους, ἀγνοοῦντες κᾀνταῦθα μέγιστόν τι θεώρημα, περὶ οὗ διελέχθην ἤδη διδάσκων ὑμᾶς ἐν τῷ λόγῳ μὲν πρῶτον ἐξεῦρον, εἶθ’ ὑπὸ τῆς πείρας ἔγνων βεβαιούμενα, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ νῦν ἀναμιμνήσκω. πολὺ γὰρ θερμοτέρων τε καὶ διαφορητικωτέρων δέονται φαρμάκων αἱ νύξεις τῶν νεύρων ἤπερ ὅταν ὅλον γυμνωθῇ, τραύματος ἀξιολόγου γενομένου. τὰ γάρ τοι γυμνωθέντα τὰς τῶν δριμέων τε καὶ θερμῶν πάνυ φαρμάκων ἐπιθέσεις οὐ φέρει διὰ τὴν δῆξιν, ἀλλὰ τῶν χαλαστικῶν καὶ διαφορητικῶν δεῖται μεμιγμένων συμμέτρως τοῖς στύφουσι φαρμάκοις. ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅτι βέλτιόν ἐστι τὰ πέριξ τοῦ τραύματος καταλαμβάνειν τοῖς δριμέσι τε καὶ λεπτομερέσι καὶ διαφορητικοῖς ἱκανῶς, ὁποῖόν ἐστι τὸ διὰ τοῦ εὐφορβίου. ἐγὼ μὲν οὖν ἐτεκμηράμην τὴν προκειμένην ἔμπλαστρον ἱκανὴν εἶναι ποιεῖν ἃ προείρηκα, καὶ σκευάσας ταύτην εὗρον τὴν πεῖραν μαρτυροῦσαν.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐμμότους, περὶ οὗ διελέχθην ἤδη διδάσκων ὑμᾶς ἐν τῷ λόγῳ μὲν πρῶτον ἐξεῦρον, καταλαμβάνειν

But since most physicians, out of ignorance, apply such plasters in a hard state, it is better to make them into lint-dressings (ἐμμότους) by melting them,  or to work them soft straightaway in the course of preparation. Then, when they have made trial of them on large divisions in which nerves have been laid bare, they expect that they will be much more beneficial in the case of punctures, being ignorant here too of a most important theorem, concerning which I have already discoursed when teaching you — things which I first discovered by reasoning and then recognised as confirmed by experience; for this reason I remind you of it now as well. For punctures of nerves require drugs that are much hotter and more discutient than when a whole nerve has been laid bare, a considerable wound having occurred. For those that have been laid bare do not tolerate the application of acrid and very hot drugs, because of the bitingness, but they need relaxing and discutient ones mixed in due proportion with the astringent drugs. But also that it is better to take hold of the parts around the wound with drugs that are acrid, fine-particled and sufficiently discutient, such as the one made with euphorbium. I, then, inferred that the plaster under discussion was capable of doing what I have said before, and having prepared it I found experience bearing witness.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἐμμότους, ἐν τῷ λόγῳ μὲν πρῶτον ἐξεῦρον (relative pronoun apparently missing; perhaps ὃ τῷ λόγῳ), καταλαμβάνειν, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὅτι βέλτιόν ἐστι (construction elliptical; governing verb supplied as 'I have taught')

Since most physicians, in their ignorance, apply such plasters hard, it is better to make them suitable for application on lint (ἐμμότους) by melting them,  or else to make them soft straight away in the preparation. Then, when they have tried them on large divisions in which nerves have been laid bare, they expect them to be much more beneficial in punctures, being ignorant here too of a very important principle, about which I have already discoursed in teaching you, which I first discovered by reasoning and then recognised as confirmed by experience; for this reason I remind you of it now as well. For punctures of the nerves need much hotter and more discutient drugs than when a nerve is laid bare entirely, a considerable wound having occurred. For nerves that have been laid bare do not bear the application of acrid and very hot drugs, because of the biting, but need relaxing and discutient drugs mixed in due proportion with astringent drugs. But I have also taught that it is better to cover the parts around the wound with drugs that are acrid, fine-particled and strongly discutient, such as the one made with euphorbium. I, then, inferred that the plaster under discussion was capable of doing what I have said above, and having prepared it I found experience bearing witness to this.

Greek (Kühn)

ὕστερον  δέ ποτε καὶ ταῖς Ἀσκληπιάδου τε καὶ Κρίτωνος ἐντυγχάνων φαρμακίτισι βίβλοις ἔγνων αὐτοὺς γεγραφότας, οὐχ ὥσπερ καὶ Ἀνδρόμαχος ἀδιορίστως, ἀλλὰ μετὰ διορισμῶν σαφῶς ἥν τινα δύναμιν ἔχει. διά τε οὖν τοῦτο καὶ διότι διαφωνοῦσιν ἐν τῇ σκευασίᾳ, κατά τινα τῷ Ἀνδρομάχῳ, κάλλιον ἔδοξέ μοι προσγράψαι καὶ τὰς ἐκείνων αὐτῶν λέξεις, ἔπειθ’ οὕτως εἰπεῖν τι καὶ περὶ τῆς ἐν τῇ συνθέσει τοῦ φαρμάκου διαφωνίας αὐτῶν. [Περὶ τῶν γεγραμμένων ὑπ’ Ἀσκληπιάδου καὶ Κρίτωνος περὶ τῆς προκειμένης ἐμπλάστρου.] Περὶ τῆς ὑπ’ Ἀνδρομάχου γεγραμμένης Αἰγυπτίας ἐμπλάστρου ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης οὕτως ἔγραψεν. Ἀνδρομάχου Αἰγυπτία ἄναιμος, τραυματική. ποιεῖ πρὸς νεύρων καὶ μυῶν διακοπὰς, ποιεῖ πρὸς νύγματα, περιθλάσεις, παρατρίματα, λυγίσματα. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ὀφθαλμιώντων παρακόλλημα. ποιεῖ κεφαλαλγικοῖς καὶ πρὸς πολλὰς διαθέσεις χρήσιμόν ἐστι τὸ φάρμακον. ♃ λιθαργύρου, κηροῦ ἀνὰ δραχ. ρμδ΄. ἀμμωνιακοῦ θυμιάματος δραχ. οβ΄. τερμινθίνης δραχ. λστ΄.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

But later  at some point, when I also came upon the pharmacological books of Asclepiades and Criton, I learned that they had written, not indefinitely as Andromachus too had done, but with distinctions, clearly, what power it has. Both for this reason, then, and because they disagree in certain points with Andromachus regarding the preparation, it seemed better to me to append the very words of those authors as well, and then in this way to say something also about their disagreement regarding the composition of the drug. [On what has been written by Asclepiades and Criton about the plaster under discussion.] Concerning the Egyptian plaster written down by Andromachus, Asclepiades wrote as follows. Andromachus' Egyptian, bloodless, wound-plaster. It is effective for severings of nerves and muscles; it is effective for punctures, contusions, chafings, sprains. It is also an adhesive application for those suffering from ophthalmia. It works for those suffering from headache, and the drug is useful for many conditions. ♃ Of litharge, of wax, 144 drachms each; of ammoniac incense, 72 drachms; of terebinth resin, 36 drachms.

Later , at some point, when I came upon the books on drugs of Asclepiades and of Crito as well, I found that they had written, not without distinctions as Andromachus did, but with distinctions, clearly what power it has. For this reason, then, and because on certain points they disagree with Andromachus in the preparation, it seemed better to me to add also their own words, and then to say something about their disagreement in the composition of the drug. [On what Asclepiades and Crito wrote about the plaster under discussion.] Concerning the Egyptian plaster written up by Andromachus, Asclepiades wrote as follows. Andromachus' Egyptian, bloodless, a wound plaster. It works for severings of nerves and muscles; it works for punctures, contusions, abrasions, sprains. It is also an adhesive application (paracollema) for those suffering from ophthalmia. It works for those with headache, and the drug is useful for many conditions. ℞ Litharge, wax, of each 144 dr.; ammoniac incense 72 dr.; terebinth 36 dr.

ἐρίων οἰσυπηρῶν κεκαυμένων ﻿  δραχ. ιη΄. λεπίδος χαλκοῦ δραχ. η΄. λεπίδος στομώματος δραχ. η΄. λιβάνου δραχ. η΄. ἀριστολοχίας στρογγύλης δραχ. η΄. κίκεως κο. γ΄. ταῦτα προειπὼν ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης ἐφεξῆς γράφει τὸν τρόπον τῆς ἑψήσεως, οὐδὲ τοῦτο τὸ μέρος τῆς συνθέσεως ἐπὶ τῶν ἐμπλάστρων φυλάξαντος τοῦ Ἀνδρομάχου, παραλέλειπται γὰρ ἐν ἁπάσαις σχεδόν. ἀλλὰ περὶ μὲν τῶν ἑψήσεων εἴρηται μὲν οὐκ ὀλίγα κατὰ τὸ δ΄. γράμμα καὶ νῦν εἰρήσεται μετ’ ὀλίγον. ἄξιον δὲ ἐπισκέψασθαι τὸ τῆς διαφωνίας, ἣν ἐν τῇ ποσότητι τῶν ἁπλῶν φαρμάκων ἐποιήσατο πρὸς τὸν Ἀνδρόμαχον ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης. οὐ γὰρ ἓξ κοτύλας τοῦ κίκεως, ὥσπερ ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος, ἀλλὰ τρεῖς ἐμβάλλειν ἀξιοῖ, κατὰ δὲ τὴν ἀριστολοχίαν οὐδὲν προδιορισαμένου τοῦ Ἀνδρομάχου, προσέθηκεν αὐτὸς τὸ στρογγύλην, ὥσπερ γε καὶ κατὰ τὸ κεκαυμένον ἔριον τὸ οἰσυπηρόν. τὸν μέντοι σταθμὸν ἴσον ἐμβάλλει τῷ Ἀνδρομάχῳ. γράφει γοῦν οὕτως. ♃ ἐρίων οἰσυπηρῶν κεκαυμένων δραχ. ιη΄. ἀριστολοχίας στρογγύλης δραχ. η΄.

of greasy, oesypum-laden wool, burnt,  18 drachmas; of scale of copper, 8 drachmas; of scale of steel (stomōma), 8 drachmas; of frankincense, 8 drachmas; of round aristolochia, 8 drachmas; of castor-oil (kiki), 3 cotylae. Having first stated these things, Asclepiades next writes the manner of the boiling—Andromachus not having preserved even this part of the composition in the case of the plasters, for it has been omitted in almost all of them. But concerning the boilings not a little has been said in the fourth book, and it will also be spoken of now, a little further on. It is worth examining the matter of the disagreement which Asclepiades made with Andromachus in the quantity of the simple drugs. For he thinks it right to put in not six cotylae of the castor-oil, as Andromachus does, but three; and in the case of the aristolochia, Andromachus having made no prior specification, he himself added 'round', just as also in the case of the burnt greasy wool. The weight, however, he puts in equal to that of Andromachus. At any rate he writes thus: ♃ of greasy wool, burnt, 18 drachmas; of round aristolochia, 8 drachmas.

Greek (Kühn)

ἃ δ’ ἐπὶ τέλει προσέθηκεν ὁ Ἀνδρόμαχος οὐδὲ ὅλως ἔγραψεν ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης, οὔτε περὶ τῆς  σμύρνης οὔτε περὶ τοῦ ὀποπάνακος εἰπών τι, πολὺ δὲ μᾶλλον οὐδὲ περὶ τῆς θαλάσσης, ἃ σαφῶς προγράψαντος τοῦ Ἀνδρομάχου, θαλάσσης εἰς ἐμβροχὴν κηροῦ δραχ. ν΄. τοῦτο γὰρ οὔτε ὁ Ἀσκληπιάδης οὔτε ὁ Κρίτων προσέγραψε. τὴν μέντοι ἀριστολοχίαν τὴν στρογγύλην ὁ Κρίτων ὁμοίως τῷ Ἀσκληπιάδῃ βάλλει, καθάπερ καὶ τῶν οἰσυπηρῶν ἐρίων τάς τε δραχ. ιη΄. ἐλαίου τε κικίνου, κατὰ λέξιν γὰρ οὕτως ἔγραψε κοτύλας στ΄. ἡ δὲ ἕψησις τῆς ἐμπλάστρου παρὰ μὲν Ἀσκληπιάδου γέγραπται τόνδε τὸν τρόπον. ἕψε λιθάργυρον καὶ κίκινον ἕως συστραφῇ, εἶτα τὰς λεπίδας, καὶ ὅταν εὔχρους γένηται, τὰ λοιπὰ ἐπίβαλλε. παρὰ δὲ τῷ Κρίτωνι τόνδε τὸν τρόπον. ἕψε λιθάργυρον μετὰ κικίνου, ἕως μέλιτος σχῇ τὸ πάχος. τούτου δὲ γενομένου ἐπίπασσε τὰς λεπίδας καὶ σύνεψε ἐφ’ ἱκανὸν, ἕως ἀμόλυντος γένηται, ἔπειτα τερμινθίνην καὶ κηρὸν, ἡνωμένοις δὲ ἀμμωνιακὸν προμαλαχθὲν παρὰ πυρὶ τοῖς δακτύλοις. βαστάζων δὲ ἤδη ἀπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς ἔμπασσε τῶν οἰσυπηρῶν ἐρίων τὴν κόνιν, μετέπειτα ἐπὶ τέλει τὸν λίβανον, εἶθ’ οὕτως ἀναιροῦ.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: θαλάσσης εἰς ἐμβροχὴν κηροῦ δραχ. ν΄, συστραφῇ

But what Andromachus added at the end Asclepiades did not write at all, saying nothing either about the  myrrh or about the opopanax, and much less about the sea-water, although Andromachus had clearly written beforehand: 'sea-water for the soaking (ἐμβροχή) of the wax, 50 drachmas'; for neither Asclepiades nor Criton added this. The round aristolochia, however, Criton puts in in the same way as Asclepiades, just as also the 18 drachmas of greasy (oesypum-bearing) wool and of castor oil—for he wrote literally thus—6 kotylai. The boiling of the plaster is written by Asclepiades in the following manner: boil litharge and castor oil until it thickens together, then the scales, and when it becomes of good colour, add the rest. In Criton, in the following manner: boil litharge with castor oil until it has the thickness of honey. When this has happened, sprinkle on the scales and boil together for a sufficient time, until it becomes non-staining; then terebinth and wax, and, once these have been united, ammoniac previously softened by the fire with the fingers. And when already lifting it from the fire, sprinkle in the ash of the greasy wool, afterwards at the end the frankincense, and then take it up thus.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: θαλάσσης εἰς ἐμβροχὴν κηροῦ δραχ. ν΄ (punctuation unclear: 'sea-water for moistening; wax 50 dr.' or 'sea-water for moistening the wax, 50 dr.'), ἐμβροχὴν, συστραφῇ, ἀμόλυντος, κόνιν, ἀναιροῦ

What Andromachus added at the end, Asclepiades did not write at all. He said nothing about the  myrrh or about the opopanax, and much less about the sea-water, although Andromachus had clearly written these down beforehand: 'sea-water for moistening; wax, 50 drachmas.' For neither Asclepiades nor Crito added this. Crito, however, puts in the round birthwort just as Asclepiades does, and likewise the 18 drachmas of greasy wool, and of castor oil, for he wrote it thus word for word, 6 kotylai. The boiling of the plaster is written by Asclepiades in this manner: 'Boil the litharge and the castor oil until it thickens, then the flakes, and when it takes on a good colour, add the rest.' By Crito it is written in this manner: 'Boil litharge with castor oil until it has the consistency of honey. When this has happened, sprinkle on the flakes and boil them together for a sufficient time, until it becomes non-staining. Then add the terebinth and the wax, and when these are united, the ammoniac, softened beforehand with the fingers by the fire. Then, lifting it from the fire, sprinkle in the powder of the greasy wool, and after that, at the end, the frankincense, and then take it up thus.'

And concerning  what the drug professes, Criton wrote more carefully, verbatim thus: "The Egyptian, which keeps every division and every contusion free of inflammation. It is also well suited to every bite of wild beasts, and it benefits also the inflammations that arise without ulcers. It is good also for pains of the nerves and muscles that arise without a manifest cause." [Another grey plaster of Andromachus.] Andromachus also wrote other grey plasters, somewhat of the same kind as the one written above. He calls the one Athena, the other nygmatic (for punctures). The Athena is suited to more conditions than the nygmatic, and especially to those accompanied by ulcers, since it is both more varied in composition and partakes still more of astringent drugs; whereas the nygmatic is more relaxing and more pain-relieving than this one. Concerning the former of the drugs mentioned, then, he wrote verbatim thus: Grey Athena. Take: litharge, 100 drachmas; terebinth resin, 36 drachmas; wax, 25 drachmas; Sinopic red earth, 24 drachmas; ammoniac incense, 36 drachmas; galbanum, 12 drachmas; aloe, 8 drachmas; diphryges, 8 drachmas.

And Andromachus wrote many other plasters together with these, adding nothing to them beyond the name of their colour, composed of drugs opposite in their powers; but some of them have the relaxing and anodyne [quality] predominating, others the discutient and detersive, and others astringency. So that we too must, having confidence in all  of them, use each suitably: for ulcers hard to cicatrize and malignant, those that contain more of the astringent; where it is proposed to soothe and relax, those [containing] the relaxing; and where to draw out and disperse, those [containing] the heating [drugs] and those which draw to themselves the vicious humours of conditions lying in the depth. To some of them this too has been added in writing: "Simple grey plaster, which I use." For he wrote three such in succession, not composed of many ingredients, and of these too I thought it better to append the composition in his own words: "Simple grey plaster, which I use. Take litharge 24 drachms, kiki (castor) oil 1 cotyle, burnt copper 4 drachms, manna 4 drachms, resin 4 drachms, Colophonian 8 drachms. Another, anodyne, of Isidorus. Take litharge 36 drachms, wax 36 drachms, oil 1½ cotylae, Colophonian resin 15 drachms, copper scale 9 drachms, manna 50 drachms. Boil. Another, which I use. Take litharge 144 drachms, dry resin 144 drachms, scale 24 drachms, oil 3 cotylae."

Andromachus also wrote, together with these, many other plasters, adding nothing else to them but the name of the colour, composed of drugs contrary in their powers. Some of them, however, have the relaxing and anodyne as the predominant quality, some the discutient and detergent, and some astringency. So we too must use all  of them with confidence, each in its appropriate way: for hard-to-cicatrise and malignant ulcers, those that have more of the astringent; where the aim is to soothe and relax, those that have the relaxing quality; and where it is to draw and discuss, those that contain heating ingredients and draw to themselves the noxious moistures of deep-seated affections. To some of them this too has been added in writing: 'Grey plain plaster which I use.' For he wrote three such plasters in succession, not of many ingredients, whose composition it also seemed better to me to add in his own wording. Grey plain plaster which I use. ℞ Litharge 24 dr., castor oil 1 kotyle, burnt copper 4 dr., manna 4 dr., resin 4 dr., Colophonian resin 8 dr. Another, anodyne, of Isidorus. ℞ Litharge 36 dr., wax 36 dr., oil 1½ kotylai, Colophonian resin 15 dr., copper flake 9 dr., manna 50 dr. Boil. Another which I use. ℞ Litharge 144 dr., dry resin 144 dr., flake 24 dr., oil 3 kotylai.

And just as to these three plasters written out above he added a note—to some that  he himself uses them, to others that it is anodyne—so too he bears witness to others, naming them theriacs. At any rate the third from the beginning is written word for word thus: "Theriac which I use." And after this another again, whose heading is as follows: "Eupatorian theriac." But about the theriacs we shall speak in what follows. Now, however, I wish to speak further about the grey plasters written by Andromachus, blaming him because he omitted to state both their professed uses and their preparations. At any rate, one of my physician friends used the one written first of all, and it accomplishes very many things. For it is cicatrizing for ulcers difficult to cicatrize, and especially when callused and hard lips surround the ulcer. It is suitable also for putrefactions and for fistulas and sinuses and large wounds, and in general it is a polychreston (of many uses). The proportion of the drugs composing it has been written as follows: ℞ litharge, 144 drachmas; oil, 5 cotylae; quicklime, 48 drachmas; frankincense, 48 drachmas; burnt copper, 48 drachmas; terebinth, 48 drachmas; aristolochia, 48 drachmas.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Εὐπατόριος, ἐπαγγελίας

Just as he added notes to these three written above, to some that  he himself uses them, to another that it is anodyne, so he gives testimony to others by naming them 'theriac'. The third from the beginning, at any rate, is written word for word thus: 'Theriac, which I use.' And after this one there is another again, whose heading is as follows: 'Eupatorian theriac.' But concerning the theriac plasters it will be said in what follows. For now I wish to speak further about the grey plasters written down by Andromachus, finding fault with him because he omitted to state both their indications and their preparations. For instance, one of my physician friends used the very first of them all as written, and it accomplished a great many things. For it is cicatrizing for ulcers that are hard to cicatrize, especially when callused and hard lips surround the ulcer. It is suitable also for putrefactions, for fistulae and sinuses, and for large wounds, and it is altogether polychrest. The proportion of the drugs composing it is written as follows: ℞ litharge 144 dr., oil 5 kotylai, quicklime 48 dr., frankincense 48 dr., burnt copper 48 dr., terebinth 48 dr., birthwort 48 dr.

of pitch ninety-six drachmas, of wax ninety-six drachmas, of ammoniac  ninety-six drachmas. Thus Andromachus wrote it. It is also possible for you to prepare it in a smaller proportion, mixing in a fourth part of each of the ingredients mentioned. For the composition will be as follows: Take litharge, 36 dr.; of quicklime, burnt copper, frankincense, terebinth and aristolochia, a third of each by weight relative to the litharge, that is, 12 dr.; then of pitch, wax and ammoniac incense, of each double the amount of the five drugs mentioned (πέμπτων), I mean the aristolochia, terebinth, frankincense, burnt copper and quicklime. The lime must evidently be unslaked and fresh; in this way the drug becomes useful also against putrefactive conditions. But since Andromachus wrote all the drugs in drachmas, he would have done better to mention the oil also according to the same proportion, stating that what is put in weighs so many drachmas; or, if not this, at any rate he certainly ought to have mentioned the Roman pound.

But as it is, he writes a kotyle, leaving us the customary  ambiguity about this measure, whether he wants the kotyle to be of nine measured ounces or of twelve. In my judgement the complete proportion should take the six ounces by weight, because the Roman litra weighs twelve ounces; and if you prepare it at the quarter, the proportion is quite evident. It is also quite evident if someone says that 480 drachms are to be taken for the complete proportion, because the weight-ounce weighs eight drachms. And there is another one too among the grey plasters written by Andromachus, this one also taking unslaked lime, of the same kind as the one written above. In order, then, that you may not seek another grey plaster in any of the other authors, I shall add two others written by Andromachus.

He calls  one of them that of Phtheirographos, which he says is compounded of these: ♃ litharge 100 dr., resin 50 dr., ammoniac incense 30 dr., wax 50 dr., scraped verdigris 12 dr., copper scale 2 dr., aristolochia 8 dr., centaury root 8 dr., oil 2 cotylae. And this drug too is sufficiently of many uses (polychrēston), just like the others  written above, and one must use all of them with confidence. Hellespontia, according to Andromachus. This plaster too, being renowned, Andromachus recorded among the grey plasters, in this proportion: ♃ litharge 100 dr., pitch 50 dr., ladanum 25 dr., scraped verdigris 8 dr., burnt copper 8 dr., scale 2 dr., old oil 200 dr. [Hellespontia of Heras.] But since Andromachus differs from Heras in the composition of the drug, it seemed good to me to add also what has been written by Heras, which runs word for word as follows. Hellespontia, diapnoic (promoting transpiration), non-inflammatory. For scrofulous swellings (choirades), phygethla (glandular swellings), parotid swellings, the splenion, once applied, draws, produces pus, breaks (them), agglutinates, and in general it acts on all burns, and it also soothes gouty patients. ♃ old oil 100 dr., dry pitch 50 dr., ladanum 25 dr., litharge 100 dr., scraped verdigris 8 dr., galbanum 4 dr. Having ground the litharge finely with the oil, boil it.

They are effective for divisions that are not great,  and for the larger wounds of the flesh itself, and the superficial bites of four-footed animals; they also disperse recent boils (dothienes). They open those that already contain pus too, and, carrying off the eschars upon them, they will very quickly bring on cicatrisation without surgery. And when applied to the forehead they alone most easily check the fluxes of those suffering from ophthalmia. Of this kind I shall first describe the better plaster, easily procured, of good colour, effective; I call it the Tyrian, for it takes on the colour of Tyrian purple, if it is made in the way it ought. Prepare it thus: of good litharge put in five pounds, and of wax one, and of pine resin three, the oily kind and free of wood.

And of good verdigris of copper and of galbanum, and of burnt copper and of good scale,  put in four ounces of each ingredient; and of rich oil, fifteen pounds and one. And boil the plaster carefully in this way: having put the very finely ground litharge together with the oil into a rather large and new earthenware pot, rub it up with the hands, then stir it also with a spatula, and boil it over a fierce and continuous flame. And when it takes on the consistency of honey, add the verdigris and the scale, and let it boil for a sufficient time; then add the copper. And having again let it boil for a short time, pour off a little of the drug from the pot; cool it and rub it up with the hands and the fingers, and when you see it well-coloured and hard, then add the pine resin and the wax. Then, when all have been carefully united, again drip off a little.

And he said to burn them in this manner: putting into a new dish or a wide-mouthed pot an abundance of greasy wool, having first smeared the body (of the vessel) with gypsum or with ash, or with potter's earth mixed with water, burning it in a fierce fire, and when it is easy (to do so),  pounding it and sifting it—at least as far as it seems to be well; and to burn them likewise also in baking-ovens, and in furnaces that have been very strongly fired, and grinding them fine in this way, take the weight that is required. He said that they are to be burnt in this manner: placing a plentiful quantity of greasy wool in a new dish or a wide-mouthed pot, having first smeared its body with gypsum or with dust, or with potter's earth mixed with water, and burning it on a fierce fire, when it is easy to do so,  pound it and sift it. It seems, at any rate, to be fine to burn them likewise also in baking-ovens and in furnaces that have been very strongly heated; and, grinding them fine in this way, take the weight that is needed. He orders that the litharge, ground very fine, be boiled together with all the castor oil, after mixing them in a new pot; when the drug has become of the thickness of honey, sprinkle in the flake and boil it together long enough, until it becomes non-staining; then add the turpentine and the wax, and then, once these are united, the ammoniac softened with the fingers. When you are about to lift the pot from the fire, sprinkle in ground frankincense, and throw in also the ash of the greasy wool at the very last, a little later, but before the frankincense.

But some, wishing this drug to be called their own, remove the one  of the one scale and put in the sixteen; and they use scale of copper, not of steel (stomōma). Another. [Περὶ τῶν διαφορητικῶν καὶ ἐπισπαστικῶν ἐμπλάστρων.] Οἱ τὰς τῶν συνθέτων φαρμάκων δυνάμεις γράψαντες ἐκάλεσάν τινας τῶν ἐμπλάστρων ἐπισπαστικὰς, ὥσπερ ἑτέρας διαφορητικάς. ἐνίαις δ’ αὐτῶν ἑκάτερον ἐμαρτύρησαν ἐπισπᾶσθαί τε καὶ διαφορεῖν. καὶ γὰρ πλησίον ἀλλήλων εἰσὶν αἱ δυνάμεις, ἥ θ’ ἑλκτικὴ τῶν ἐκ τοῦ βάθους, ἥ τε διαφορητικὴ τῶν ἑλχθέντων. αἵ τε γὰρ ἕλκουσαι καὶ διαφοροῦσί τι πάντως, αἵ τε διαφοροῦσαι καὶ ἕλκουσιν. ἀλλ’ ἐπι κρατεῖ κατὰ μὲν τὰ διαφορητικὰ τῶν φαρμάκων ἡ διαφορητικὴ δύναμις, κατὰ δὲ τὰ ἑλκτικὰ ἡ ἑλκτική.

Boil the oil, the scale, and the litharge until it comes together and becomes very good in colour and a sufficiently hard plaster; then, in this way, throw in the terebinth and the wax, then  the ammoniac softened with the fingers; then, having lifted the pot from the fire while it is boiling, you will then throw in the manna, when the drug is already cooling. I have often made this without litharge, throwing in an equal quantity of good molybdaena, and have used it with the greatest satisfaction for the purposes we mentioned before, and made of a better colour. [On the diaphoretic and epispastic plasters.] Those who have written on the powers of compound drugs called some of the plasters epispastic, just as they called others diaphoretic. And to some of them they attested both, that they both draw and disperse. For indeed the powers are close to one another, the attractive power that draws things from the depth, and the diaphoretic power that disperses what has been drawn. For those that draw also disperse something in any case, and those that disperse also draw. But in the diaphoretic drugs the diaphoretic power prevails, and in the attractive ones the attractive.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Πεποιημένην τε χρώματος βελτίονος, ἐπι κρατεῖ

Boil the oil, the scale and the litharge until it comes together and becomes of a very good colour, and a sufficiently hard plaster; then, in this way, put in the terebinth and the wax, then  the ammoniac softened with the fingers; then, lifting the pot boiling from the fire, you will then put in the manna, the drug already cooling. I have often made this without the litharge, putting in an equal amount of good molybdaena, and used it most gladly for the things we mentioned before. And it was made of a better colour. [On dispersing and attractive plasters.] Those who wrote on the powers of compound drugs called some of the plasters attractive, just as others dispersing; and to some of them they attested both, that they draw and that they disperse. For the powers are close to one another: the power that draws things from the depth, and the power that disperses what has been drawn. For those that draw also certainly disperse something, and those that disperse also draw. But in the dispersing drugs the dispersing power prevails, and in the drawing drugs the drawing power.

The attractive ones, then, are composed by means of those simple drugs which  have the power to draw the surpluses of the humours from the depth to the skin, while the discutient ones are composed from those which are of a nature to disperse what has been drawn. But in order that a plaster may result, as was said in what precedes, with one of them litharge is mixed, with another resin, with another wax, with another oil, with another pitch—such things having neither a noteworthy discutient power nor an attractive one, as do propolis or lime or marrow or galbanum or the flower of the Asian stone or foam of nitron or aphronitron or vinegar or dripped lye, pepper and mustard and soapwort, and both the edible kinds of salts and the ammoniac ones, and in addition to these many others, concerning whose power it has been stated in the treatise on simple drugs. But now I shall state the plasters that have already given sufficient proof by experience, saying only this much more about them: that some have also mixed some of the astringent drugs with the epispastic ones, guarding against what happens in those cases in which there will be a surplus of humours.

For they sometimes draw more than is fitting, so that they produce the attraction not only from what lies in the depth at the part under treatment, but also from all the  surrounding parts. And I have said before, in the discussion of the flesh-producing powers, that the attractive and the discutient ones, just as also all those that cleanse more than they should, become flesh-producing by admixture of cerate—of more cerate in those whose power is strong, of less in those whose power is weak. But if the compound attractive or discutient drug happens to contain also something astringent, one must not use such a drug as a flesh-producing one, because the astringent powers are useful for the agglutination of wounds and of sinuses and for the cicatrization of ulcers, but useless for the production of flesh. Rx: of terebinth, of Asian stone, of asphalt, 360 drachms each; of aphronitron, of calf's (fat), of wax, of bay-berries, of ammoniac incense, 340 drachms each; of pyrite stone 80 drachms; of quicklime 108 drachms, otherwise 1 mina; of ash of asps 140 drachms; of old oil 2 cotylae. It is also a good emollient (malagma),  as Antonius the root-cutter used it. It is effective for the affections about the joints. Boil the salt and white lead, ground very fine, with the oil, and stir until it does not stain; and when it is in good condition, having taken it off the  fire, add the chalcanthum, which has been ground fine with vinegar, sprinkling it on little by little and continuously, and taking care that it does not boil up; then, having poured it out into a mortar and beaten it up, take it up and use it. [The discutient (plasters) written down by Asclepiades, in his own wording.] When the swellings have been dissolved and have resolved into fluid, the preparations written below are to be used. Cerate of Ctesiphon, for scrofulous swellings, ganglia, parotid swellings and the abscesses on the breasts; like no other of the drugs it both dissolves the swellings and draws off all the fluid lying nearby through the rarefied passages, and it glues together sinuses. It works for fistulas and aegilopes, so as to deliver (the patient) without incision. It is also a good malagma for sciatic, gouty and arthritic patients, when applied after the paroxysms, so as to free them from the whole condition. The matters of its preparation are as follows. Take wax, 1 litra; terebinth, 1 litra; nitron, 1 litra; old oil, 1 litra; water, 1 kotyle.

[The discutient (plasters) written by Asclepiades, in his own words.] When the swellings have been dissolved and given off into fluid, the preparations written below are to be used. The wax plaster of Ctesiphon, for choirades, ganglia, parotids and the abscessions in the breasts: like no other of the drugs it both dissolves the swellings and carries off all the adjacent fluid through the porosities, and it glues together the sinuses. It is effective for fistulae and aegilopes, so as to rid the patient of them without incision. It is also a good emollient for sciatic, gouty and arthritic patients, applied after the paroxysms, so as to rid them of the whole affection. The particulars of the preparation are as follows. ℞ Wax, 1 litra; terebinth, 1 litra; nitre, 1 litra; old oil, 1 litra; water, 1 kotyle.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἄγγει, ψόφος, ξυλώδους

Grind nitron and water, so that it is completely dissolved, and having put it into an earthenware vessel and set it  on the fire, boil it, having covered the vessel with a lid and stirring at intervals. When the loud noise has ceased, add the meltable ingredients, stirring continuously until it no longer stains; having emptied it into a mortar and beaten it up, take it up and use it. Or prepare it in this manner: the meltable ingredients are melted, and when they have dissolved, we add to them the nitron ground fine with the water, adding it little by little and stirring continuously and taking care that it does not boil over. Another, which Menecrates used, specifically for scrofulous swellings and for the hardnesses on the breasts; for it perforates them and discharges the surrounding fluid, and glues together the sinuses and brings them to a scar. ℞ wax, 1 litra; terebinth resin, 1 litra; woody resin, 1 litra; galbanum, 1 litra; old oil, 1 litra; aphronitron, 1 litra; ash of myrrh, 1 xestes, which comes to (ἄγγει) 3 ounces.

Put the resins into a bronze or earthenware vessel, set it on the fire and melt them, and having dissolved out of these as much as suffices to dissolve the galbanum, then, having mixed the meltable ingredients together, boil until it no longer stains; then, taking it off the fire, let it cool, and add to these  aphronitron, and stirring continuously and having beaten it up, add it to the ashes, ground as fine as possible, and having mixed them together and taken it up, use it, undoing the dressing every fifth day, then every third day, until the parts around the swellings have been perforated, then undoing it daily, carry on the treatment. Another, the one with aphronitron, called by some Dionysia, a well-proven drug for the aforesaid conditions, which Largus used. Take wax 1 litra, terebinth 1 litra, manna 1 litra, aphronitron 1 litra, oil 1 xestes, potter's water (pēlopoiikon) 1 xestes, raw misy 2 ounces. Compound it in the manner (described) (συντίθει καὶ τρόπον). Another, the one with Asian stone, a well-proven drug for the aforesaid conditions. Take wax 1 litra, terebinth 1 litra, nitron 1 litra, frankincense half a litra, flower of Asian stone half a litra, henna oil half a litra, potter's water as much as suffices. The dry ingredients are dissolved in potter's water so as to have the thickness of strigil-grease (gloios), and are ground thoroughly. The meltable ingredients are melted and cooled and added to the ground ingredients; having beaten it up and taken it up, we use it. Another, the Asian one, of the Neapolitan; it is effective for the aforesaid conditions.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ῥυπῶδες, Πλατυνθεὶς, Τὸ πολὺ

It is prepared thus: of good litharge, at most a litra; of white lead, half a litra. To these one adds a litra of fresh  rich oil. Having put in the litharge, boil it with the oil, and when you have made it like a salve in thickness, then add the white lead, finely ground. But when you see that it does not stain and is very well-tempered, and a little harder, add wax and pine-resin; and when these have melted, lift the pot, still boiling, from the fire, and then put in the ammoniac, flattened out with the hands and fingers, as wax customarily is beside the fire; then, stirring the drug with the spatula and kneading it again with the hands, store it away thus. Another. Another for all these conditions; it disperses scrofulous swellings more effectively, and benefits those who suffer, having apprehension of an abscess of the hypochondrium together with some swelling and fear. Being black, it has this preparation: of liquid pitch and of dry, two litrai each.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ῥυπῶδες, Πλατυνθεὶς, διῢγρου, Τὸ πολὺ

It is prepared thus: of fine litharge at most a litra, of white lead a half-litra; to these he adds a litra of new  rich oil. Having put in the litharge, boil it with the oil and, making it dirty-looking in its thickness, then add the white lead, ground smooth. When you see it non-soiling and very well tempered, rather a little harder, throw in wax and pine resin; and when these have melted, lifting the pot boiling from the fire, then put in ammoniacum, flattened with the hands and the fingers beside the fire, as wax usually is; then stirring the drug with the spatula, and again softening it with the hands: in this way store it away. Another. Another for all these things; but it disperses scrofulous swellings more, and it benefits sufferers who are apprehensive of an abscession of the hypochondrium accompanied by some swelling and fear. Being black, it has this preparation: of very moist pitch and of dry pitch, 2 litrae.

Greek (Kühn)

Τούτοις παραχέας ὄξος εὐῶδες, βραχὺ Λέαινε ἐφ’ ἱκανὸν ἐν θυείᾳ μείζονι,  Κηρῷ δὲ ῥητίνῃ τε προσβαλὼν βραχὺ Ἔλαιον, τήξας ἐπὶ πυρὸς λάβρου φλογὸς, Μιγνύς τ’ ἐπιμελῶς πάντα καὶ μαλάγματα Ποιῶν τ’ ἀμολύντου τὸ πάχος, ἔχ’ ἀποκείμενον. Χρώμενος ἐφ’ ὧν δεῖ προσφάτῳ τῷ σπληνίῳ. Ἔλεγε δ’ ὁ Κράτιστος καὶ πατὴρ τοῦ φαρμάκου, Καὶ πρὸς νόσων τε τῶν μακρῶν ἀπαλλαγὰς, Εὔχρηστον εἶναι καὶ καλὸν τὸ φάρμακον, Ἐφ’ ὧν τε χρόνιος φλεγμονὴ σπλάγχνων τινὸς Ὑποχόνδριον πεποίηκε σκληρὸν ἢ μέγα Σφόδρ’ ὠφελεῖν, ἱδρῶτας γὰρ ἐπιφέρει Καὶ τὰς σκληρίας λύει τῶν ὑποχονδρίων, Ἀπαλλαγάς τε τῶν νόσων ποιεῖ ταχὺ, Καὶ τὰς ἐποχὰς δὲ τῶν φυσικῶν καταμηνίων Ἄγει βραχείαις ἡμέραις ἐπικείμενον. Ἐγὼ δ’ ἀφαιρῶν τὴν τίτανον, στυπτηρίαν Διπλῆν ἔβαλλον καὶ τὸ φάρμακον ποιοῦν Ἄμεινον ἔγνων, οὐ ποιοῦν τὰ ψυδράκια.

Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: Κράτιστος, ἀμολύντου

Pouring fragrant vinegar over these, a little, grind them for a sufficient time in a larger mortar,  and adding a little oil to the wax and the resin, melting them over a fire of fierce flame, and mixing everything carefully and making a malagma of the thickness of a non-staining plaster, keep it stored away, using it, in the cases where it is needed, with a fresh compress (splenion). And the most excellent man and father of the drug used to say that the drug is serviceable and good also for deliverance from long diseases, and that in those cases where a chronic inflammation of some viscus has made the hypochondrium hard or large it helps greatly; for it brings on sweats and dissolves the hardnesses of the hypochondria, and quickly effects deliverance from the diseases, and it also brings down retentions of the natural menses when applied for a few days. But I, removing the lime, put in double alum, and I found the drug acting better, not producing the small pustules (psydrakia).

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: βραχὺ, ἐφ’ ἱκανὸν, λάβρου φλογὸς, ἀμολύντου, Κράτιστος, σπληνίῳ, ψυδράκια

Pouring fragrant vinegar over these a little at a time, grind them sufficiently in a larger mortar;  and adding a little oil to the wax and the resin, melting them over a fire of vigorous flame, and mixing everything carefully, and making emollients (malagmata) of the thickness of the amolyntos ('non-soiling' plaster), keep it in store, using it, in the cases where it is needed, on a fresh pad. The most excellent man, the father of the drug, said that the drug is also serviceable and good for the riddance of long diseases, and that in those in whom a chronic inflammation of one of the viscera has made the hypochondrium hard or large it helps greatly; for it brings on sweats and dissolves the hardnesses of the hypochondria, and quickly effects the riddance of the diseases; and the suppressions of the natural menses it brings on when it has lain applied for a few days. But I, removing the 'lime' (titanos), used to put in double the alum, and found the drug acting better, not producing the pustules (psydrakia).

Greek (Kühn)

 ΓΑΛΗΝΟΥ ΠΕΡΙ ΣΥΝΘΕΣΕΩς ΦΑΡΜΑΚΩΝ ΤΩΝ ΚΑΤΑ ΓΕΝΗ ΒΙΒΛΙΟΝ Η. Μαλάγματα καλοῦσιν οἱ ἰατροὶ τὰ τῶν ἐσκληρυσμένων σωμάτων παρὰ φύσιν μαλακτικὰ, θερμῆς μὲν ἅπαντα κράσεως ὄντα μετρίας, οὐ μὴν ξηραίνοντά γε, καθάπερ οὐδὲ ὑγραίνοντα σαφῶς. μέσα γάρ πως ὄντα τά γε ἀκριβῶς μαλακτικὰ τῶν ὑγραινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων, οἷον χύσιν τινὰ τῆς οὐσίας τῶν σκληρυνομένων ἐργάζεται.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)

 GALEN, ON THE COMPOSITION OF DRUGS BY KIND, BOOK 8. Physicians call malagmata those things that are emollient of bodies that have been hardened contrary to nature, all of them being of a moderately hot mixture, yet not drying, just as they do not clearly moisten either. For those that are precisely emollient, being somehow intermediate between things that moisten and things that dry, produce, as it were, a certain diffusion of the substance of the parts that are being hardened.

Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: Η (book number in the heading: Η is 'eighth', although the chapter numbering points to Book VII), χύσιν

 GALEN, ON THE COMPOSITION OF DRUGS BY KIND, BOOK VIII. Physicians call malagmata ('emollients') those drugs that soften bodies which have been hardened contrary to nature. All of them are of a moderately hot krasis, yet they are not drying, just as they are not clearly moistening either. For those that are emollient in the strict sense are in some way intermediate between moistening and drying agents, and they bring about, as it were, a kind of melting of the substance of the parts that are being hardened.
